| v5.8.26 | **ANSI colors in the terminal (SGR) — `ls --color` + a colored editor status bar.** Extends the v5.8.25 CSI parser with **`ESC[…m` (SGR)**: `terminal_capture_putchar` now tracks a `cur_color` (a VGA attribute byte, fg = low nibble / bg = high nibble) updated by `term_apply_sgr` — `0` reset, `1` bold→bright fg, `7` reverse, `30-37`/`90-97` set fg, `40-47`/`100-107` set bg (via an ANSI→VGA map). Every written cell takes `cur_color`: screen-mode cells directly, and scrollback via a new per-char `out_color[]` buffer committed by `term_add_line_c` — so a colorized line **keeps its colors in history**. The draw now renders the **background** too (`vga_to_rgb` on the high nibble; previously bg was always black), and the block cursor inverts fg/bg. Two demos: **`ls.elf`** emits `ESC[1;34m` for directories and `ESC[1;32m` for `*.elf` (reset after each), and **`edit.elf`**'s status bar is drawn black-on-light-grey (`ESC[30;47m` + `ESC[K` to paint the whole row). Verified (`exec /ls.elf`): every `.elf` renders **bright green** in the GUI (dirs would be blue). Verified (`exec /edit.elf`): the status bar is a solid **black-on-grey** bar (classic nano look) — proving background color. init.elf regression **PASS**, 0 panics. No new syscall (still 29). |
| v5.8.27 | **Visual refresh — unified crescent-moon logo (login + `nyxfetch`) + updated GIFs.** The `nyxfetch` moon ASCII art moved into a shared **`kernel/nyx_logo.h`** (single source of truth); `nyxfetch` now references it instead of a local copy. The **login screen** gained that same moon, drawn in brand **purple** above the login box — each glyph row is rendered over the matching gradient color (`login_grad`) so it blends into the background instead of sitting on a flat panel; the "NyxOS Login" title is tinted purple to match. The README's `media/startup.gif` (login-with-moon → desktop → nyxfetch) and `media/terminal.gif` (nyxfetch → colored `ls` → `ps`) were regenerated from fresh QEMU captures on the v5.8.26 color terminal. Verified in QEMU (0 panics): the login screen shows the purple moon blended into the gradient, and `nyxfetch` renders the identical moon with cyan info labels — the brand mark is now consistent across the boot/login/shell surfaces. Display-only change (no kernel logic touched). |
| v5.8.28 | **Shared ELF libc — one copy of libc, mapped into every process.** Instead of statically bundling ~14 KB of libc into each of 21 programs, libc is now built ONCE as a **prelinked shared object** (`user/libc.so`, `ld -Ttext 0x30000000`) that the kernel loads at boot (`shared_libc_load`, new `kernel/shared_libc.c`) and maps into every user address space (`shared_libc_map`, called from `elf_load_image`). Its read-only `.text`/`.rodata` are mapped **shared** — the same physical frames in every process, RO (a new `map_page_ro` in paging.c, since `map_page_dir` forces writable), `page_incref`'d so process teardown's refcount-aware `free_page` balances and `fork`'s `clone_page_directory_cow` shares them for free; libc's one writable page (`.bss` = the malloc freelist head) is a **private per-process copy**. Programs link with **`ld --just-symbols=libc.so`** (no PLT/GOT, no dynamic linker) so their direct calls to `printf`/`malloc`/... resolve to libc's fixed addresses at 0x30000000 (within rel32 range of the program at 0x10000; the sbrk heap cap moved below it). Result: ELFs shrank ~35% (`init.elf` 26 KB→**18 KB**, `ls.elf` 15 KB→**9.9 KB**), libc ships once (10.6 KB). Verified (`exec /init.elf`, 0 panics): the full suite — `fork` (COW over the shared RO libc + private `.bss`, cow_test isolation intact), `execve`→42 (re-maps libc), signals→143, `mmap`, `getprocs`, `/proc`, `readkey` — all **PASS**; `ls.elf` malloc's + lists correctly. No new syscall (still 29). |
| v5.8.29 | **`dlopen`/`dlsym` — runtime dynamic loading.** Extends the v5.8.28 shared-library machinery from "libc, mapped into everyone at boot" to "any `.so`, loaded on demand by a process." Two syscalls: **`SYS_DLOPEN` (29)** — `dlopen(path)` loads the prelinked `.so` (once, into shared master frames) and maps it into the **calling** process's address space (`do_dlopen`, `kernel/shared_libc.c`), returning a handle; **`SYS_DLSYM` (30)** — `dlsym(handle, name)` resolves a symbol to its address by walking the library's `.symtab`/`.strtab` (new `elf64_shdr_t`/`elf64_sym_t` in elf.h). shared_libc.c was refactored into reusable `libseg_load`/`libseg_map` (shared by libc and the dynlib registry). Because libraries are prelinked at fixed VAs there is **no relocation** — dlsym returns the symbol's link-time `st_value`, the caller casts it to a function pointer and calls it. Demo: **`libdemo.so`** (prelinked at 0x31000000, self-contained `demo_gcd`/`demo_fib`/`demo_name`) + **`dltest.elf`**, which does NOT link libdemo — it loads it at runtime. Verified (`exec /dltest.elf`, 0 panics): `dlopen(/libdemo.so)`→handle 1, `dlsym` resolves all three functions, and calling them gives `demo_gcd(48,36)=12`, `demo_fib(10)=55`, `demo_name()="libdemo v1"`; init.elf regression still **PASS**. mkinitramfs now ships every `.so`. |
| v5.8.30 | **`/proc/<pid>/maps` — a process's mapped memory regions.** A new generated `/proc/<pid>/maps` node (built on the v5.8.22 `/proc` machinery — no new syscall) lists the address ranges present in a process's page tables, each with permissions and a label. A new `vm_collect_regions` (paging.c) walks the user half of the address space and coalesces runs of present pages with the same perms into `vm_region_t{start,end,writable,exec}` (a COW page counts as writable); `proc_generate` formats them `START-END rwx [label]`, labelling by VA — `[program]`, `[libc]` (the shared libc), `[dynlib]` (dlopen'd), `[mmap]`, `[stack]`. So `cat /proc/<pid>/maps` shows exactly what's mapped, including the shared libc and any dlopen'd library. **Also fixes a kernel `snprintf` bug**: `%x`/`%lx` formatted via `itoa((int)val)`, truncating 64-bit values to 32 bits (and ignoring width) — rewritten to be 64-bit-safe with zero-padding, so 48-bit VAs print correctly. Verified (`exec /init.elf`, 0 panics): `/proc/5/maps` shows `000000010000-000000012000 rwx [program]`, the three shared-libc segments at `0x30000000` (`r-x`/`r--`/`rw-`), and the stack at `7fffffffe000-7ffffffff000 rw- [stack]`; init.elf suite still **PASS**. |
| v5.8.31 | **`pmap` + `free` — userspace tools over `/proc`.** Two small coreutils that read the generated `/proc` files (pure userspace, no kernel change). **`pmap [pid]`** (`user/pmap.c`) reads `/proc/<pid>/maps` (default: itself via `getpid`), prints each region, and parses the hex ranges to report a total (`8 regions, 36 KB mapped`). **`free`** (`user/free.c`) reads `/proc/meminfo` and prints the classic `total/used/free` table. Both are ~10 KB — they link against the shared libc (`--just-symbols`), and `pmap` of itself even shows the shared libc mapped at `0x30000000`, tying the last few increments together. Verified (0 panics): `exec /free.elf` → `Mem: (kB) 261631 total / 184 used / 261447 free`; `exec /pmap.elf` → its program/libc/stack regions + the summary. |
| v5.8.32 | **`env` / `date` / `sleep` — coreutils + real environment inheritance.** Three userspace tools plus the plumbing that makes `env` meaningful. **`date`** reads the RTC through a new `SYS_TIME=31` (fills a 6-int `{sec,min,hour,mday,mon,year}` buffer — a stable, padding-free ABI over the kernel's `rtc_time_t`) and prints `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS`. **`sleep N`** blocks on a new `SYS_SLEEP=32` (`do_sleep_ms`): the caller parks `PROC_BLOCKED` with a `wake_tick` on the timer wait queue and the scheduler runs others until the deadline — the same mid-syscall discipline as `waitpid` (`blocked_in_kernel` → resume on the kernel CR3, `user_cr3`/`user_rsp` saved/restored), and a signal cuts it short with `-EINTR`. **`env`** prints the process environment — which now actually exists: **`execve` carries `envp`**. `SYS_EXECVE` copies the `envp` array in like `argv` (16 entries × 159 chars); `do_execve` lays out the SysV entry stack as `[argc][argv…][NULL][envp…][NULL]`; **crt0** derives `environ = &argv[argc+1]` and publishes it as the libc `environ` global (new `getenv` reads it); and the shell seeds `USER`/`HOME`/`SHELL`/`PATH`/`TERM` and passes `env_build()` to every `execve`, so children inherit the environment and `export` propagates. No relocation surprises — programs are `-mcmodel=small` non-PIE, so the cross-module `environ` reference resolves as an absolute 32-bit reloc via `--just-symbols` (verified in the ELF: `environ` at `0x30002000`). Verified in the GUI (0 panics): `exec /date.elf` → `2026-07-12 17:30:10` (matches the taskbar clock); in `sh`, `env` → `USER=nyx`/`HOME=/home/user`/`SHELL=/sh.elf`/`PATH=/`/`TERM=nyx`, `export FOO=bar` then `env` adds `FOO=bar`, and `sleep 2` blocks ~2 s then `exit(0)` while the desktop stays responsive. 33 syscalls. |
| v5.8.33 | **`$PATH` command resolution — the environment becomes _functional_.** Closes the loop on v5.8.32: the shell now has a real `PATH` in its environment, so `resolve()` (user/sh.c) searches it instead of hardcoding `/`. A bare command name is looked up by walking each `:`-separated directory of `$PATH` in order — trying `<dir>/<name>` then `<dir>/<name>.elf` (NyxOS executables carry the suffix) and taking the first that `open()`s — so **`$PATH` is authoritative**: a command whose directory isn't on `$PATH` is `not found` (exit 127), with no silent fallback to `/` (that fallback survives only for a totally unset `$PATH`, so the shell still works bare). A name containing `/` stays an explicit path (never searched), per POSIX. The default `PATH` is now `/bin:/usr/bin:/` (the executables live at `/`, so the search walks the two empty dirs and resolves at the last entry — exercising multi-directory lookup). Pure userspace, no kernel/syscall change (still 33). Verified in the GUI (0 panics): `echo hola` → `hola` (resolved via the `/bin`→`/usr/bin`→`/echo.elf` walk); `export PATH=/tmp` then `echo hi` → `sh: echo: not found` / exit 127 (proving `$PATH` is consulted, not bypassed); `export PATH=/` then `echo recovered` → `recovered` (resolution is live — changing `$PATH` changes what runs). |
| v5.8.34 | **Terminal scrollback — PgUp/PgDn + mouse wheel + a scrollbar.** The terminal keeps 2000 lines of history but always showed the tail; now you can navigate it. The draw path already honored `scroll_offset` (and `term_add_line` bumps it to hold the view steady when output arrives while scrolled) — this wires the input side. `terminal_win_key` (kernel/terminal_win.c) gains a `term_scroll()` helper (clamped to `[0, line_count - visible_rows]`, no-op in TUI screen mode) and handles **PgUp/PgDn** (one screenful minus a row of overlap; the keyboard already emitted `KEY_PGUP`/`KEY_PGDN`) and synthetic **`KEY_WHEEL_UP`/`KEY_WHEEL_DOWN`** (3 lines); any other key snaps back to the live tail. A **scrollbar** on the right edge shows the thumb sized to `visible/total` and positioned by scroll depth — dim at the tail, purple while scrolled. **Mouse wheel** required real driver work (kernel/mouse.c): the PS/2 mouse was a plain 3-byte device, so `mouse_enable_wheel()` does the IntelliMouse "magic knock" (sample rate 200→100→80, then read device ID; `0x03` = wheel) and the IRQ handler now reads a **4-byte packet** whose signed low-nibble Z accumulates into `mouse_z`; the compositor diffs `mouse_get_z()` each loop and injects one `KEY_WHEEL_*` per notch to the focused window (and its wheel state joins the idle-yield wake condition). Verified in QEMU (0 panics): boot logs `[MOUSE] Initialized (wheel)`; `help` fills the screen, **PgUp** scrolls to `Available commands:` at the top (thumb rises + turns purple), **PgDn** returns to the tail; injecting wheel notches via the monitor's `mouse_move dx dy dz` scrolls the view (thumb tracks) and plain moves still track the cursor correctly (4-byte parse intact). No syscall change (still 33). |
| v5.8.35 | **More coreutils — `cp` / `mv` / `uname` / `whoami` / `which` / `clear` (+ a real build-system fix).** Six small userspace tools (each links the shared libc, so ~10 KB): **`cp SRC DST`** and **`mv SRC DST`** stream a file in 4 KB `.bss`-buffered chunks (`mv` = copy then `unlink`, since ring 3 has no rename syscall); **`uname`** prints `NyxOS` (or the full `/proc/version` banner with `-a`); **`whoami`** reads `$USER` via `getenv`; **`which NAME…`** reimplements the shell's `$PATH` resolver and prints where each name would run (closing the v5.8.33 loop); **`clear`** emits `ESC[3J`. That needed one tiny terminal change (kernel/terminal_win.c): `ESC[3J` now erases the scrollback (`line_count = 0`) **without** entering full-screen mode, so `clear` composes cleanly inside the userspace shell (plain `ESC[2J` still means a TUI screen-clear). **Also fixes a latent build bug** `uname` surfaced: the makefile's `%.o: %.c` rule didn't depend on `kernel.h`, so a bare `KERNEL_VERSION` bump never recompiled `vfs.o`/`kernel.o` — `/proc/version` (and nyxfetch) had been stuck at 5.8.30 across five releases. Adding `kernel.h` as a prerequisite fixes version propagation. Verified in the shell (0 panics): `whoami`→`nyx`, `uname -a`→`NyxOS version 5.8.35 (x86_64)` (now correct), `which whoami`→`/whoami.elf`, `cp /home/user/welcome.txt /w` + `cat /w` round-trips the content, `mv /w /w2` + `cat /w2` confirms the move, `clear` wipes the screen and the shell keeps working. No syscall change (still 33). |
| v5.8.36 | **Job control — Ctrl-Z stop + `jobs`/`fg`/`bg` with transparent restart.** Real Unix job control. **Kernel:** a new `PROC_STOPPED` state (the scheduler already only runs `PROC_RUN`, so it's skipped for free); `SIGTSTP` (20, Ctrl-Z) and `SIGSTOP` **park** the process instead of terminating — `signal_dispatch` (or, for a blocking syscall, the new `signal_check_stop`) sets it `PROC_STOPPED`, wakes the parent via `wake_waiters`, and `sti/hlt`s until `SIGCONT` flips it back to `PROC_RUN` (the do_waitpid mid-syscall discipline: `blocked_in_kernel` + saved `user_cr3`/`user_rsp`). `do_waitpid` gains `WUNTRACED` — it reports a stopped child with the sentinel status `WSTOPPED|sig` (bit 16, unambiguous vs. exit codes). A new `SYS_SETFG` (33) lets the shell point terminal signals at its foreground child; keyboard **Ctrl-Z → SIGTSTP**. **The key subtlety:** a job stopped mid-syscall must *resume* its wait, not abort — so the blocking syscalls (`sleep`, stdin read/readkey) call `signal_check_stop` (park + restart) and consume the `SIGCONT` that woke them, so e.g. `sleep` keeps sleeping after `bg`. `cmd_exec` auto-resumes a Ctrl-Z'd direct-exec job (the kernel terminal has no job control). **Shell (v0.8):** a jobs table (pid/stopped/cmd); `jobs`, `fg [%n]` (SIGCONT + setfg + wait), `bg [%n]` (SIGCONT); the foreground path `setfg`s the child, waits `WUNTRACED`, and parks a Ctrl-Z'd job; it ignores SIGTSTP at the prompt. Verified in the GUI (0 panics): `sleep 30` → **Ctrl-Z** → `[1] Stopped sleep 30`, `jobs` lists it, `bg 1` → `jobs` shows `[1] Running sleep 30` (it *keeps running*, not killed) — proving stop + resume + restart. 34 syscalls. |
| v5.8.37 | **Shell globbing (`*`/`?`) + `~` expansion.** The shell now expands wildcards and home. **`~`** (at a word boundary, followed by `/`, space, or end) expands to `$HOME` in `expand_vars`, so `cd ~` and `echo ~` → `/home/user`. **Globbing** is a new pass in the forked child (user/sh.c): after `split_args`/`parse_redir`, `expand_globs` walks each argv token — an arg with `*`/`?` is replaced by the directory entries it matches (`getdents` on the token's directory, a small recursive `glob_match` where `*` = any run and `?` = one char; leading-dot names are skipped unless the pattern starts with `.`, and a no-match arg stays literal — nullglob off). Because a glob like `*.elf` can expand to many files, the kernel's **execve argument cap was raised 8 → 32** (`kargv_store[32]`, `uargv[33]` in do_execve). Pure userspace + one kernel limit bump; no new syscall (still 34). Verified in the shell (0 panics): `echo *.elf` → `args.elf cat.elf clear.elf cp.elf date.elf dltest.elf echo.elf edit.elf env.elf …` (9+ entries — the raised cap in action), `echo ~` → `/home/user`, `cd ~` + `pwd` → `/home/user`. |
| v5.8.38 | **Wallpaper app — a color-picker for the desktop background (purple by default).** The desktop background was a hardcoded blue/green gradient; now it's a **selectable base color**, defaulting to the NyxOS brand **purple (morado)**. New `kernel/wallpaper_win.c` holds an 11-color palette (`Morado`, `Azul`, `Turquesa`, `Verde`, `Lima`, `Oro`, `Naranja`, `Rojo`, `Rosa`, `Pizarra`, `Carbon`) and a picker window (a 4-column swatch grid, the current color framed white) modeled on the Settings window (`window_create` + `on_click`, content origin `win->x, win->y+TITLE_H`). `draw_background` (compositor.c) was rewritten to build a smooth vertical gradient from `wallpaper_base_color()` — brightness scaled ~45%→115% top-to-bottom. The app is launched from the desktop **right-click menu** (new 5th item "Wallpaper"); clicking a swatch sets the base color and the compositor repaints the background live. Verified in the GUI (0 panics): the desktop boots **purple**; right-click → Wallpaper opens the picker (Morado selected); clicking **Rojo** turns the whole desktop red instantly. No syscall change (still 34). |
| v5.8.39 | **Shell operators — `&&` / `\|\|` / `;` command lists.** The userspace shell (user/sh.c) grew a layer *above* pipelines: a command line is now a list of pipelines joined by `;` (sequence — always run the next), `&&` (run the next only if the last succeeded, `$?==0`) and `\|\|` (run the next only if it failed). A new `split_list` splits the line in place on the top-level operators — carefully **not** mistaking `&&` for a background `&` or `\|\|` for a pipe `\|` (only the doubled forms are list operators; a lone `&`/`\|` is left inside the segment for the existing background/pipeline splitter). A new `run_command_list` drives them: each segment is var-expanded **on its own** (so `$?` sees the *previous* segment's status), then dispatched as a builtin or a pipeline via `run_line`. Short-circuiting works because a skipped segment leaves `last_status` untouched — exactly bash's behaviour for `a && b \|\| c` chains. Two tiny built-ins were added, `true`/`false` (bash has them too), purely to set `$?` for testing. `main` and `sh -c` both route through `run_command_list`, and the `demo` script now exercises the operators. Pure userspace; **no new syscall (still 34)**. Verified in the GUI terminal via `exec /sh.elf` (0 panics, output read off the serial mirror): `true && echo …`→ runs, `false \|\| echo …`→ runs, `false && echo skipped ; echo …`→ prints only the `;` branch (no "skipped"), `false; echo $?`→ `1`; a live-typed `true && echo liveok`→ `liveok` (the raw-mode readline preserves the operator chars end-to-end). |
| v5.8.40 | **Double-buffered compositor — the desktop no longer flickers.** The GUI drew straight to the visible linear framebuffer: `fb_fill_rect`/`fb_put_pixel`/the cursor all wrote to the LFB directly, so every `redraw_all()` painted the full-screen background first, then icons, then each window one at a time — and the user *saw* that sequence, worst of all during clicks/drags (which recomposite every event) → visible flicker. Fixed with **double buffering** (kernel/fb.c): the compositor calls the new `fb_enable_backbuffer()` at startup, which allocates an off-screen buffer the size of the framebuffer and redirects `fb_addr` (the target of every `fb_*` primitive *and* the cursor code, via `fb_get_addr`) to it; each finished frame is then blitted to the hardware LFB in one `memcpy` via the new `fb_present()`. So the screen swaps atomically from one *complete* frame to the next — the half-drawn intermediate states are never visible. The back buffer is **opt-in**: it's off during bootsplash/login (which draw once, straight to the LFB, needing no present) and only turns on when the compositor starts; if the ~3 MB allocation ever fails it cleanly falls back to direct rendering. `redraw_all()` sets a `frame_dirty` flag so *any* caller (incl. menu/drag handlers that call it directly) gets published; the event loop presents once per iteration only when `frame_dirty || pointer-moved` (so an idle desktop doesn't needlessly blit); `compositor_redraw_now` (the foreground-exec repaint path) presents itself. Kernel-only, no syscall change (still 34). Verified in QEMU (0 panics): boot logs `[FB] double buffering on — back buffer 1024x768 (3072 KB)`; the desktop, all windows, taskbar and cursor composite correctly; the pointer moves and the taskbar clock tick present cleanly; opening the Start menu (the heaviest `redraw_all`: background + icons + every window + the menu overlay) paints atomically with no corruption. |
| v5.8.41 | **Graphical kernel panic — a visual stop screen instead of a silent freeze.** Before, `kernel_panic()` only `printf`'d to the VGA text buffer + serial, so in GUI mode a panic just froze the desktop with no on-screen indication. Now it renders a full-screen **NyxOS-purple stop screen**: a big `:(` face, "KERNEL PANIC", the formatted reason, the register/fault context (CR0/CR2/CR3 + the `__builtin_return_address` caller), a restart footer, and a "NyxOS" brand mark. Mechanics: `kernel_panic` now `cli`s, formats the message once into static storage via a new **`vsnprintf`** (factored out of `snprintf`), logs to serial as before (still greppable `[KERNEL PANIC]`), then calls a new static `panic_screen()`. Because the compositor's double-buffer present loop is dead by panic time, `panic_screen` calls the new **`fb_use_lfb_direct()`** (kernel/fb.c) to repoint drawing straight at the hardware LFB, then paints with `fb_fill_rect` + a new scaled font routine **`font_draw_string_scaled()`** (kernel/font.c — the font is a fixed 8x16, so each pixel becomes a scale×scale block for the big face/heading). It's best-effort: it only draws when a framebuffer is up (text-mode boots keep the serial log). The exception handler (isr.c) now passes a descriptive reason — `"<name> (#n) at RIP 0x… (ring r, err 0x…)"` — so real #PF/#GP/#UD faults show the exception name and faulting RIP, with CR2 as the fault address. Kernel-only, no syscall change (still 34). Verified in QEMU (SDL + monitor screendump): `crash` → the desktop is replaced by the purple stop screen showing `:(`, "KERNEL PANIC", "Reason: User requested crash", `CR0=0x80010011 CR2=0x0 CR3=0x1000 from=0x10611d`, and the restart footer; serial logs `[KERNEL PANIC] User requested crash`. |
| v5.8.42 | **Shell command substitution `$(cmd)` (+ two latent kernel bugs it exposed).** `$(command)` now runs `command` in a forked subshell and splices its captured stdout into the line before parsing. **Shell (user/sh.c):** a new `expand_cmdsubst()` pre-pass (run on the whole line inside `run_command_list`, before the `;`/`&&`/`\|\|` split) finds each `$(...)` (closing paren matched by depth), and `capture_command()` runs the inner text via `run_command_list` in a `fork`ed child with fd 1 `dup2`'d to a pipe, draining the pipe in the parent; the captured text is trailing-trimmed and its internal newlines/tabs become spaces (unquoted word-splitting). Nesting works because the subshell runs the inner text back through `run_command_list`. Verified: `echo working dir is $(pwd)` → `working dir is /home/user`, `echo nested: $(echo $(echo deep))` → `nested: deep`, live-typed `echo dir $(pwd)` → `dir /home/user`. **Two latent kernel bugs the feature surfaced, both fixed:** (1) the ring-3 **user stack was a single 4 KB page** — the shell's larger command-list/substitution frames overflowed it and #PF'd on the *first* command; now every user process gets **`USER_STACK_PAGES`=16 (64 KB)**, mapped growing down from `0x7FFFFFFFF000` in `elf_load_image` and the `create_user_process` fallback. (2) a process's **fds were closed only at reap, not at exit**, so `$(cmd)`'s parent blocked forever reading the subshell's pipe (the zombie still held the write end → no EOF); `SYS_EXIT` now calls `close_proc_fds()` (made non-static) so pipe write ends drop at exit and readers get EOF — this also makes pipeline EOF fire sooner (no regression). No new syscall (still 34); shell banner v0.11. Verified in the GUI terminal (0 panics), and the full `demo` — pipelines, operators, background jobs — still runs end-to-end. |
| v5.8.43 | **Shell quoting — `'...'` and `"..."`.** Quoted words can now hold characters that are otherwise special to the parser: spaces (no word-splitting), the operators `; & \| < >`, and the glob/expansion chars `* ? ~ $ ( )`. Userspace-only (user/sh.c), no kernel change (still 34 syscalls). Rather than teach every splitter about quotes, a new `preprocess_quotes()` pre-pass (run first in `run_command_list`, before `$(...)` and the operator split) strips the quote marks and rewrites each *protected* character to a **control-byte placeholder** (0x01–0x11) that none of the existing splitters/expanders look for; the real bytes are restored by `q_unprotect()` only when the final argv / redirect filename / `cd`/`export` arg is built. **Single** quotes protect everything (fully literal — `$`/`(`/`)` too, so nothing expands); **double** quotes leave `$ ( )` live so `$VAR` / `$?` / `$(...)` still expand inside them. A `$(...)` is copied verbatim by the pre-pass (its inner command keeps real spaces; the subshell re-parses it). Verified in the GUI terminal (0 panics): `echo "hello world"` → one arg `hello world`; `echo 'a; b'` → literal `a; b` (the `;` is not an operator); `echo "$HOME"` → `/home/user` (expands); the `demo` also shows `"…a; b \| c"` staying literal, `'…$HOME…'` literal, and `"…$NAME"` expanding. **Known limitation** (no quoting rewrite of the splitters can fix cheaply): the *output* of an unquoted-context `$(...)` is still word-split even inside `"..."`. ⚠️Observed once during testing: a **transient ring-3 #PF in a pipeline stage panics the kernel** (the long-standing "unhandled ring-3 fault panics instead of killing the process" limitation) — not caused by quoting (a no-op for that line) and non-deterministic (re-ran clean); a proper `#PF → SIGSEGV` for ring 3 is the real fix, tracked as future work. |
| v5.8.44 | **Ring-3 fault isolation — a crashing program no longer panics the kernel.** Before, ANY CPU exception taken from ring 3 (segfault, illegal instruction, div-by-zero, …) fell through to `kernel_panic` — one buggy user program took down the whole OS (the flaky pipeline panic seen in v5.8.43 was exactly this). Now `isr_handler` (kernel/isr.c) checks the fault's privilege level: **`(cs & 3) == 3`** (fault from ring 3) → terminate just that process with the default action of the signal it earned (`exception_signal()` maps #DE→SIGFPE, #UD→SIGILL, #MF/#XM→SIGFPE, everything else incl. #PF/#GP→SIGSEGV): set `exit_code = 128 + signo`, `close_proc_fds()`, `state = PROC_ZOMBIE`, `wake_waiters()`, then `sti` + `hlt`-forever so the scheduler takes over and never resumes the faulting instruction — the exact same teardown SYS_EXIT uses (the exception runs on the process's kernel stack via TSS RSP0, so it works identically). A fault from **ring 0** is still a genuine kernel bug → the visual `kernel_panic` (unchanged). The demand-paging fast path (`vm_handle_fault` for COW / lazy-heap / mmap) still runs first, so only *genuine* faults kill. New test program **user/fault.c** (`fault.elf`, NULL-deref) makes it reproducible. No new syscall (still 34). Verified in QEMU (0 kernel panics): running `fault` in the shell logs `[fault] pid 6 (fault): Page Fault (#14) at RIP 0x10017 err 0x6 fault-addr 0x0 -> killed (signal 11)`, the shell reaps it with `exited with status 139` (=128+11=SIGSEGV) and a subsequent `echo alive` → `alive` proves the shell (and the rest of the system) survived the child crash. This retires the "unhandled ring-3 #PF panics" known-latent bug. |
| v5.8.45 | **Pager — `less` / `more`.** A full-screen userspace pager (user/less.c → both `less.elf` and `more.elf`, same program). It reads a file (`less FILE`) or, with no arg, **stdin** (so `cmd \| less` works), buffers it, splits into lines, and shows a screenful at a time on the terminal's cursor-addressed screen mode (`ESC[2J` + `ESC[r;cH`, like edit/top) with a grey status bar (`name  ln a-b/total  pct%  [keys]`). Nav keys come from **`readkey()` — the keyboard, independent of fd 0** — which is the trick that makes paging a pipe work even though stdin is the pipe: Space/`f`/PgDn page down, `b`/PgUp page up, `j`/`k`/arrows line, `g`/`G` top/bottom, `q`/ESC quit. ANSI colors in the content pass through (e.g. `ls --color \| less` shows dirs blue, `.elf` green). No new syscall (still 34); shell banner unchanged. ⚠️**Debugging note that matters:** the first cut buffered 128 KB (~176 KB `.bss`); `ls / \| less` then **reliably** crashed while `ls / \| grep` never did — because a large program image makes `execve()` slow, which **widens the window for the pre-existing IRQ-load Heisenbug** in the forked pipe-stage child (fault appeared as `q_unprotect(-1)` in the sh child at a *consistent* RIP, then a follow-on ring-0 panic). Shrinking `less` to a 32 KB buffer (~48 KB `.bss`) narrowed the window enough that `ls / \| less` is now clean 3/3. Verified in QEMU (0 panics): `less /home/user/welcome.txt` renders the file + `~` fillers + status bar and `q` returns to the shell; `ls / \| less` pages 50 lines (Space → ln 23-44/50, colors intact) and quits cleanly, shell survives. The underlying Heisenbug is still latent (real fix = the register-corruption issue, separate work) — keeping user programs small avoids tripping it. |
| v5.8.46 | **Identity map now covers all RAM (was a fixed 64 MB) — fixes the pipeline kernel-panic root cause.** Root-caused the intermittent `cmd \| less`-class crash with QEMU `-d int`: the kernel identity-maps only the low **64 MB** (`IDENTITY_MAP_MB`), but `alloc_page()` hands out physical pages across **all** RAM (511 MB under `-m 512`), and the kernel touches every page-table / COW target page it allocates through that page's **identity (low) virtual address** (`map_pml4`, `pte_ptr`, `memset` of a fresh table). Under memory pressure — a large program's `.bss` + fork COW + a pipeline — `alloc_page()` eventually returned a page **above 64 MB**, and the kernel `#PF`'d dereferencing its unmapped identity address (the captured fault: `memset` writing to exactly `0x4000000` = 64 MB, and `map_pml4` reading a table above the map). Fix (kernel/paging.c `init_paging`): size the identity map to `memory_total` (rounded to 2 MB, one PD = 512 × 2 MB = 1 GB cap) instead of a constant 64 MB — since `init_paging` runs after `init_memory`, and `MAX_PAGES` already caps allocation at 512 MB, the map now always covers the allocatable range. Verified with `-d int`: boot logs `Identity-mapping 512 MB (RAM 511 MB)`; the worst-case repro (a deliberately 176 KB-`.bss` `less` piped from `ls`) went from **kernel-panicking 2/2 → 0 kernel panics** across heavy hammering, and normal `ls \| less` renders and quits cleanly. ⚠️A rarer **residual** remains under *extreme* back-to-back pipeline churn: a stage occasionally faults on a not-present shared-libc page and is killed (`[fault] … (less): … -> killed (signal 11)`) — but thanks to v5.8.44 that's **non-fatal** (the kernel and shell survive), and normal use is clean. Root-causing that shared-libc/execve-under-churn path is future work. |
| v5.8.48 | **Fix pipeline-corruption root cause #1 — pin the shared-libc master frames un-freeable.** The pipeline Heisenbug's main driver: a per-process shared-libc RO mapping wasn't matched by an `incref`, so teardown (`free_page_directory`) drove the master frame's refcount to 0 and `free_page` released it — then `alloc_page` re-handed the still-mapped frame out (double-allocation), corrupting page tables/data. Caught red-handed with per-page free-tracking + a pin (`free_page` reaching the release path for a shared-libc master at refcount 1). Fix (memory.c `page_pin` + `shared_libc.c libseg_load`): mark the masters un-freeable and floor their refcount at 1 — correct, since they live for the OS lifetime. Plus fail-fast guards: `map_pml4` full `PTE_ADDR_MASK` (was `& ~0xFFF`, leaving NX/reserved bits → non-canonical `#GP`) + reserved-bit panic, and a `slab_alloc` free-list validator. Measured: `ls \| less` fault rate ~1/3 → ~1/6. ⚠️A **separate** `-1` wild-writer remains under extreme fork+execve churn (`pstorm` reproducer; needs record/replay reverse-debug) — root cause #2, still open. (v5.8.47 "16 KB stack canary" was a red-herring — reverted; the canary never tripped.) |
| v5.8.49 | **Catchable CPU-exception signals — `signal(SIGSEGV/SIGFPE/SIGILL, handler)` recovers instead of dying.** A ring-3 fault no longer *always* terminates the process: `isr_common` now passes the exception frame to `isr_handler` (`mov r8, rsp`, 5th arg), which calls new `signal_deliver_fault(frame, signo)` (signal.c) — if the process installed a handler for that signal it diverts ring 3 into it via the same trampoline path as `signal_dispatch` (save the faulting context to `sig_saved`; rewrite RDI=signo / RIP=handler / user-RSP=adjusted-stack-with-trampoline; point `user_cr3` at the faulting pd so `copy_to_user` lands the trampoline), then `isr_handler` returns so `isr_common` `iretq`s into the handler on the process's own CR3. No handler (SIG_DFL) / SIG_IGN / a fault inside a handler / an unwritable user stack → default action (terminate as before). New libc **`setjmp`/`longjmp`** (naked, in the shared libc) let a handler jump out — the saved context is the *faulting instruction*, so returning normally would re-fault. No new syscall (reuses SYS_SIGNAL + SYS_SIGRETURN; still **34**). Demo `user/segv.c` → `segv.elf`: installs a SIGSEGV handler, derefs NULL, `longjmp`s back, **exits 0**. Verified in QEMU: `exec /segv.elf` → "caught signal 11 and recovered … exiting 0" (`code 0`), while `exec /fault.elf` (no handler) still `[fault] … -> killed (signal 11)` (`code 139`), 0 panics. (One-shot: `longjmp` out of a handler leaves the signal blocked — `siglongjmp`/multi-recovery is future work.) |
| v5.8.50 | **Panic-screen polish + kernel `%x` de-doubled.** (1) The kernel `printf` (screen.c) auto-prepended `0x` to `%x`/`%X`, so every format string that *also* wrote a literal `0x` produced **`0x0x…`** (panic registers, `[fault]` lines, driver logs) and it uglified hex dumps / MAC octets (`0x52:0x54`). Made `%x` **standard C** (no auto-prefix — now matches `vsnprintf` + the userspace libc, which were already standard; that's why the visual panic screen was single-`0x` but the *serial* wasn't), added an explicit `0x` to the handful of bare-`%x` address sites (`[MODULES]` / `[INITRAMFS]` / `[PAGING] CR3`), and switched MAC/hex-dump octets to `%02x`. Result: `0x0x` count **0** system-wide; `[KERNEL PANIC] … CR0=0x80010011 CR2=0x0 CR3=0x1000 from=0x1061ca`; `[RTL8139] MAC: 52:54:00:12:34:56`. (2) The graphical panic screen now draws the **shared NyxOS purple moon** (`nyx_logo.h`, tint `fb_rgb(168,138,245)` — same art as login/boot) in the top-right with a "NyxOS" label, replacing the plain corner text. Verified in QEMU (`crash` command): clean single-`0x` registers + the moon on the deep-purple ":(" stop screen. |
| v5.8.51 | **Network byte order consolidated into `htons`/`htonl`/`ntohs`/`ntohl` + an `IP4_OCTETS` display macro (hardening refactor — behavior-preserving).** The stack's per-protocol byte swaps were all *correct* (each a hard-won past fix) but open-coded inline — `((x<<8)&0xFF00)|((x>>8)&0xFF)` and uglier 32-bit `seq`/`ack` OR-chains — duplicated ~40× across ip/tcp/udp/dns/arp/icmp/ethernet/dhcp, the exact pattern that had produced every past wire bug. Added four `static inline` helpers to kernel.h (`__builtin_bswap16/32`, each compiling to a single `bswap`) and swept every protocol onto them: TCP ports/seq/ack/offset_flags/window/checksum (send + receive + RST paths), IP total_len/checksum, UDP ports/length, ICMP id/seq, DNS id/flags/qdcount/ancount (dropped the local `bswap16` macro), ARP htype/ptype/oper, Ethernet ethertype. Also added `IP4_OCTETS(ip)` — expands a network-order IPv4 into four `%d` printf args, first octet first — replacing the ~10 copies of `ip&0xFF, (ip>>8)&0xFF, …` in ifconfig/ping/dns/setip/dhcp/nyxfetch/tcptest. IP↔wire *byte serialization* (arp sender/target IP, the TCP pseudo-header) is deliberately left as explicit byte writes — it's a serialize, not a swap. Zero behavior change by construction (`htons(x)` ≡ the inline swap; the macro ≡ the same four args); builds clean under `-Wall -Wextra` (incl. `-Wformat`, which validates the macro's arg count/types). Verified in QEMU (ISO boot, headless slirp): full DHCP lease `OFFER/ACK IP=10.0.2.15 mask=255.255.255.0 gw=10.0.2.2`, `ifconfig` renders `lo 127.0.0.1` / `eth0 10.0.2.15`, ARP resolves 10.0.2.2 + 10.0.2.3 — desktop stable, no panic. (ping-to-gateway and DNS-upstream go unanswered under headless slirp — an environment limit, not the stack; the DHCP lease proves the same UDP/IP/Ethernet path works both directions.) Kernel-only, no syscall change (still 34). |
| v5.8.52 | **Userspace TCP sockets — `socket()`/`connect()`/`read()`/`write()`/`close()` wired to the real TCP stack, plus a built-in loopback echo service.** The socket layer (net.c) was a set of no-op stubs; it's now a thin layer over tcp.c. Two new syscalls — **SYS_SOCKET (34)** and **SYS_CONNECT (35)** — plus socket fds that live in the *same* per-process fd table as files/pipes, tagged `UFD_SOCK_FLAG (0x20000000)`, so `read`/`write`/`close` route to `tcp_recv`/`tcp_send`/`tcp_close` (BSD `send`/`recv` are libc aliases of `write`/`read`). `connect()` drives the 3-way handshake to ESTABLISHED and a socket `read()` blocks on `tcp_recv` until data or EOF — both busy-poll `kernel_poll_net()` inside the syscall (the dhcp/dns pattern; safe because `cmd_exec`'s foreground wait parks the compositor in `sleep()` and never polls, so the running program is the sole poller — no `kernel_poll_net` re-entrancy). fork doesn't inherit socket fds (like VFS handles — avoids a double-close), and `close_proc_fds` grew a socket branch so an exiting program's sockets are torn down. To give a userspace client something fully self-contained to talk to (loopback, no NIC/host), added a tiny always-on **inetd-style TCP echo service on port 7** (`tcp_echo_init`/`tcp_echo_poll`, driven from the net poll loop). New demo **user/sockdemo.c**: `socket → connect(127.0.0.1:7) → write → read → close`. **36 syscalls.** Verified in QEMU (ISO, headless): `exec /sockdemo.elf` → `connected (fd=3)`, `sent 29 bytes`, `got echo (29 bytes): "hello from userspace sockets!"`, `OK — echo matched`, exit 0, no panic. (Fixed mid-bring-up: `nsock_send` returned tcp_send's on-wire segment length, not the payload count, so `write() != len` tripped a false "write failed" → early exit → RST/retransmit noise; a socket write() now reports payload bytes.) Server-side `bind`/`listen`/`accept` syscalls and UDP (SOCK_DGRAM) sockets are the natural follow-up. |
| v5.8.53 | **Server-side userspace sockets — `bind()`/`listen()`/`accept()` (a userspace program can now run a TCP server).** Three new syscalls — **SYS_BIND (36)**, **SYS_LISTEN (37)**, **SYS_ACCEPT (38)** — over the v5.8.52 socket layer: `nsock_bind` records the local port, `nsock_listen` does a `tcp_listen` passive-open, and `nsock_accept` busy-polls `tcp_accept` until a client's handshake completes, then hands the connection out as a **new fd** (the listener fd stays open). **39 syscalls.** New demo **user/srvdemo.c**: `socket → bind(9000) → listen → (self-connect) → accept → read → write(echo) → client read`. Verified in QEMU (ISO, headless, 3/3 runs): `listening on port 9000 (lfd=3)` / `client connected (cfd=4)` / `accepted the client (afd=5)` / `server got "ping from the client" (20 bytes) → echoing` / `client got reply` / `OK`, exit 0, no panic. **Deliberately single-process** (client + server in one program via sequential loopback calls, no fork): a first cut used `fork()` so a parent server accept()ed while its child connect()ed, but that has **both sides busy-polling `kernel_poll_net()` concurrently**, which reliably tripped the latent IRQ-load register/CR3 Heisenbug (non-deterministic ring-0 instruction-fetch #PF in the net-poll code). Adding a `cli`/`sti` re-entrancy guard to `kernel_poll_net` made it worse — it fired the SAME crash **deterministically** at a fixed RIP right after the `sti` (a strong repro clue: the bug is a timer IRQ interrupting ring-0 kernel code — see [[nyxos-pipeline-corruption]]). So the guard was removed and the demo kept single-process; concurrent multi-process network polling stays unsupported until that Heisenbug is fixed. fork still doesn't inherit sockets; `close_proc_fds` tears a listener down like any socket. |
| v5.8.54 | **Scheduler fix — a process preempted mid-syscall now resumes on the correct CR3 (a latent crash the busy-polling socket syscalls newly exposed).** `irq_scheduler_tick`/`sched_target` (process.c) chose the resume CR3 from `!blocked_in_kernel`, but that flag is only set at *explicit* blocking points (waitpid/sleep/pipe). A userspace program busy-polling `kernel_poll_net()` **inside a syscall** (socket connect/recv/accept, v5.8.52+) never sets it, so a timer preemption while it ran the syscall's low `-mcmodel=large` code resumed it on its **user CR3** — which doesn't map that low kernel `.text` — → a ring-0 instruction-fetch `#PF`. Fix: pick the resume CR3 from the **ring of the interrupted frame** (saved CS at the irq_common frame offset 144 == index 18) instead of the `blocked_in_kernel` proxy — ring 3 → the proc's own CR3, ring 0 (any syscall, busy-poll or not) → the kernel CR3. Root-caused via a *deterministic* repro: a `cli`/`sti` re-entrancy guard in `kernel_poll_net` concentrated the pending timer IRQ to fire right after the `sti`, crashing `sockdemo` **every boot** at a fixed RIP (e.g. 0x111208); with the fix it passes. Verified: process-model self-tests (`init.elf`: fork/pipe/execve/dup2/waitpid/mmap/shell `&&`/`\|\|`/`;`) + `sockdemo`/`srvdemo` all pass, no panic. **Distinct from two still-open Heisenbugs** (see [[nyxos-pipeline-corruption]]): (a) *concurrent* multi-process net polling still garbles TCP state (socket demos stay single-process), and (b) the `pstorm` `-1` wild-writer (pipeline root cause #2, ~1/3 on pstorm 40). No syscall change (39). |
| v5.8.55 | **Userspace UDP sockets — `socket(SOCK_DGRAM)` + `sendto()`/`recvfrom()` over the existing udp.c.** Two new syscalls — **SYS_SENDTO (39)** and **SYS_RECVFROM (40)** — round out the socket API (**41 syscalls**). `nsock_create` now accepts `SOCK_DGRAM`, allocating a small per-socket datagram ring (`UDP_QUEUE` × `dgram_t`, kmalloc'd, freed on close); `bind()` records the local port; `sendto(fd, buf, len, flags, ip, port)` `udp_send`s (auto-binding an ephemeral source port on the first send if unbound) and reports payload bytes; `recvfrom(fd, buf, len, flags, *ip, *port)` busy-polls until a datagram is queued, returning it plus the sender's network-order IP and host-order port. Inbound delivery: `udp_handle_packet` now calls **`nsock_udp_deliver(dst_port, …)`** first — a datagram to a socket-bound port enqueues on that socket's ring; if no socket claims the port it falls back to the kernel listeners (dhcp/dns) unchanged. `connect()` is now TCP-only (rejects `SOCK_DGRAM`). New demo **user/udpdemo.c**: `bind(9001)` a receiver, `sendto` a datagram to 127.0.0.1:9001 from a second socket, `recvfrom` it. Verified in QEMU (ISO, headless): `sent 14 bytes … received "hello over UDP" (14 bytes) from 127.0.0.1:48000`, exit 0, no panic; the TCP demos (`sockdemo`/`srvdemo`) still pass. Single-process/loopback like the TCP demos — concurrent multi-process net polling is still the open issue (see [[nyxos-pipeline-corruption]]). |
| v5.8.56 | **`nc` (netcat) — a userspace tool that bridges stdin/stdout to a TCP or UDP socket, plus a UDP echo service to talk to.** Ties the whole socket API together in one program (`user/nc.c`): `nc <ip> <port>` (TCP client), `nc -u <ip> <port>` (UDP client), `nc -l <port>` (TCP server — bind/listen/accept). Half-duplex (send a stdin chunk, print one response) since NyxOS has blocking sockets and no `select()` — right for request/response protocols (echo, HTTP/1.0). **No new syscalls** (reuses socket/connect/bind/listen/accept/sendto/recvfrom + read/write; still 41). Also extended the built-in loopback echo service to answer **UDP as well as TCP** on port 7 (RFC 862): a `udp_echo_handler` registered as a kernel udp listener bounces each datagram back (it only fires when no userspace socket claims port 7, since `nsock_udp_deliver` runs first). Verified in QEMU via the shell pipe: `exec /sh.elf`, then `echo TCPNCOK \| nc 127.0.0.1 7` prints `TCPNCOK` and `echo UDPNCOK \| nc -u 127.0.0.1 7` prints `UDPNCOK` — nc connects/sends/receives and the echo bounces the payload back, both pipeline stages exit 0, no panic. (Works in a pipeline because only nc polls the net — the peer/echo is in-kernel, so there's no concurrent multi-proc net polling.) |
| v5.8.57 | **`sigsetjmp`/`siglongjmp` — multi-fault recovery (finishes the catchable-signals work from v5.8.49).** A handler that `longjmp`s out (instead of returning through the sigreturn trampoline) never runs `SYS_SIGRETURN`, so `sig_active`/`sig_mask` stayed set — the signal was left **blocked**, and a *second* fault of the same signal hit `if (p->sig_active) return 0` in `signal_deliver_fault` and killed the process (the "one-shot" limitation noted in `segv.c`). Fix: one new syscall **SYS_SIGPROCMASK (41)** — `do_sigprocmask(how, set, oldset)` reads/changes the blocked mask and, on unblock, clears the corresponding `sig_active` bit (we're abandoning that handler's stack frame). libc gains `sigsetjmp(buf, savesigs)` (a macro: stash the mask via sigprocmask, then `setjmp` in the *caller's* frame so the jump lands at the sigsetjmp site) and `siglongjmp(buf, val)` (restore the mask, then `longjmp`). So a siglongjmp out of a handler UNBLOCKS the signal and the same fault is catchable again. **42 syscalls.** New demo **user/sigrec.c** catches the SAME SIGSEGV three times (NULL deref → handler → siglongjmp → refault). Verified in QEMU: `exec /sigrec.elf` → `caught SIGSEGV #1/#2/#3, recovering` → `recovered from 3 SIGSEGVs -- multi-recovery works!`, exit 0; the one-shot `segv.elf` (v5.8.49) still passes, no panic. |
| v5.8.58 | **`alarm()` + SIGALRM — timer-driven signals.** One new syscall **SYS_ALARM (42)** — `alarm(seconds)` sets a per-process tick deadline (`p->alarm_tick = tick_count + seconds*1000`; 0 cancels) and returns the whole seconds left on any previous alarm. `irq_scheduler_tick` (which runs on the live 1000 Hz timer — irq0 does `tick_count++`) posts SIGALRM via `signal_raise` (which wakes a blocked process) once the deadline passes; delivery then runs on the process's next syscall return (`signal_dispatch`). With no handler installed, SIGALRM's default action terminates the process, like Unix. `alarm_tick` is a new zeroed proc field, so `fork()` correctly clears the child's alarm. **43 syscalls.** libc gains `alarm()`. New demo **user/alarmdemo.c**: install a SIGALRM handler, `alarm(5)` then `alarm(1)` (confirming the ~5s remaining return), sleep, catch the alarm. Verified in QEMU: `exec /alarmdemo.elf` → `previous alarm had ~5s left` / `SIGALRM delivered!` / `alarm()/SIGALRM works!`, exit 0, no panic. |
| v5.8.59 | **Persistent filesystem — verified end-to-end + polished** (the writable ext2 stack already existed; this makes it non-destructive, turnkey, and observable). NyxOS already had the full persistent stack — `ata_write_sectors`, ext2 `write_file`/`create_file`/`mkdir`/`unlink`/`alloc_block`/`alloc_inode` + superblock/BGD sync, and VFS mount integration (`mount_backed` nodes flushed to disk) that **auto-mounts a raw ext2 disk (drive 0) at `/mnt` on boot**. Verified across a reboot: `write /mnt/f`, `mkdir /mnt/d`, `rm` all persist — a fresh boot on the same disk still shows the files. Three fixes/additions: **(1)** the boot used to **write `/mnt/os-test.txt` to the user's disk on every boot** (a leftover bring-up self-test) — removed; the mount is now non-destructive and prints free space. **(2)** New **`df`** command — total/used/free 1K-blocks + inodes from the ext2 superblock. **(3)** New **`make disk`** target (mkfs.ext2 → `ext2-test.img`, classic 1024-byte blocks / 128-byte inodes) so persistence is turnkey — boot `qemu … -hda ext2-test.img`; `make clean` never touches it. No syscall change (still 43). Verified in QEMU (`-hda` a fresh disk): mounts `/mnt` writable with no stray test file, `df` shows `16384 1K-blocks, 3% used`, and `write /mnt/persist.txt` survives a reboot (`cat` → `REBOOT_SURVIVOR`). |
| v5.8.60 | **`poll()` — I/O multiplexing over sockets, pipes, and stdin (the full-duplex / multi-fd primitive).** One new syscall **SYS_POLL (43)** — `poll(struct pollfd* fds, int nfds, int timeout_ms)` waits until any listed fd is ready (POLLIN readable / POLLOUT writable), the timeout elapses (0 = non-blocking, <0 = forever), or a signal fires (-EINTR); returns the count of ready fds. Readiness is checked per fd type via new helpers: `nsock_readable` (net.c — TCP `recv_len>0`/closed via `tcp_recv_ready`, UDP datagram queued), `pipe_readable` (pipe.c — buffered data or all-writers-closed EOF), `keyboard_has_input` (keyboard.c — a stdin key/serial byte waiting), and VFS files are always ready. **44 syscalls.** libc gets `poll()` + `struct pollfd` + `POLLIN`/`POLLOUT`. New demo **user/polldemo.c**: poll a pipe + a TCP echo socket together, drain each as it becomes readable, then a poll of the idle socket times out. Verified in QEMU: `pipe readable → "from the pipe"`, `socket readable → "from the socket"`, `poll → 0 (timeout)`, `OK`, exit 0, no panic. (Fixed mid-bring-up: the poll wait must `sti` so the 1000 Hz timer advances `tick_count` — syscalls run with interrupts masked, so the timeout deadline would never pass otherwise; safe now that v5.8.54 handles mid-syscall preemption.) |
| v5.8.61 | **`nc` is now full-duplex (built on v5.8.60's `poll()`).** The TCP client and `-l` server bridge is rewritten from half-duplex (send a stdin chunk, read one response) to a **`poll()` loop over stdin (fd 0) + the socket**, forwarding each direction as data arrives — so nc can send and receive *simultaneously* (interactive chat, or a server pushing data unprompted), not just request/response. Once stdin hits EOF it drains the socket for a 500 ms grace period then exits (the loopback echo never closes; a real peer's FIN ends the socket read). **No new syscalls** (still 44); userspace-only change (`user/nc.c`, reusing `poll`/`read`/`write`). The UDP path is unchanged. Verified in QEMU via the shell: `exec /sh.elf`, then `echo TCPNCOK \| nc 127.0.0.1 7` prints `TCPNCOK` and `echo UDPNCOK \| nc -u 127.0.0.1 7` prints `UDPNCOK` — the round-trip still works through the new poll-driven bridge, both pipeline stages exit 0, no panic. |
| v5.8.62 | **The GUI file manager can now browse (and edit) the persistent `/mnt` ext2 filesystem.** The file manager already used the fd-based VFS (`vfs_open`/`vfs_readdir`/`vfs_read`, which transparently route mount paths to ext2), but three VFS gaps kept `/mnt` out of reach: (1) `/mnt` was **only a mount-table entry, never a ramdisk node**, so a listing of `/` (which walks a directory's children) never showed it — and the file manager has no address bar to type a path; (2) `vfs_rename` and (3) `vfs_cp`'s source read walked **only the ramdisk tree**, so rename / copy silently failed on mount-backed files. Fixes, all in `kernel/vfs.c`: `vfs_mount` now creates a ramdisk **stub directory node** for the mount point (`ensure_mount_stub`) so `/mnt` appears when browsing `/` — `vfs_open("/mnt")` still resolves to ext2 because `vfs_find_mount` takes precedence, so the stub only supplies the parent's entry and is never itself opened; `vfs_rename` is mount-aware (if either endpoint is on a mounted FS it does copy-then-`unlink`, for files — a directory on a mount is left intact, since that needs a recursive copy, and `vfs_cp` fails cleanly on it); `vfs_cp` loads the source and writes the destination **generically**, so copy works in all four ramdisk↔`/mnt` directions (a directory source is refused via a `readdir` probe). **No new syscalls** (still 44); kernel-only, and the file-manager GUI code is unchanged. Verified in QEMU two ways: (a) over the shell, whose `ls`/`cp`/`mv` call the very same `vfs_*` — `ls /` now lists `mnt/`, `ls /mnt` shows the ext2 root, and ram→mnt / mnt→mnt / mnt→ram `cp` plus `mv` (a `/mnt` rename) all succeed and read back; (b) in the GUI, opening the File Manager and pressing End+Enter navigates into `/mnt`, showing `lost+found/` + `hello.txt` + `renamed.txt` — the files the shell run had just written to the same disk image, so they survive across separate boots. |
| v5.8.63 | **The GUI file manager opens files in the Text Editor (cross-app integration).** Double-clicking a file — or selecting it and pressing Enter — in the File Manager now opens it in a **Text Editor** window pre-loaded with its contents, including files on the persistent `/mnt` ext2 mount (building on v5.8.62). Wiring, no new syscalls (still 44), GUI-only: (1) new `compositor_open_editor(const char* path)` in `compositor.c` factors out the editor-window creation that the start-menu / desktop "Text Editor" action used (that action now just calls it with `path = NULL` for a blank document); given a path it calls (2) the new public `editor_load_file(ed, path)` in `editor_win.c` — a thin wrapper over the editor's existing internal loader (`editor_open`), which reads the file through the VFS (so it works for ramdisk **and** mount-backed `/mnt` files). (3) The file manager detects a **double-click** (a second click on the same row within 400 ms, tracked by new `last_click_tick`/`last_click_idx` fields) and calls `compositor_open_editor`; a single click still selects the file and previews its first bytes in the status bar, and Enter on a selected file opens it the same way. The new editor window is created focused, so keyboard input flows to it immediately. Verified in QEMU (screenshot): opened the File Manager, End+Enter into `/mnt`, then **double-clicked `hello.txt`** → a Text Editor window opened titled `/mnt/hello.txt`, showing its content `Type 'help' for commands.` with the status line `Opened: /mnt/hello.txt (26 bytes)`. |
| v5.8.64 | **Persistent user accounts on the ext2 disk — `useradd` survives a reboot; the misleading "EXT2 not available" fallback is fixed.** The persistence machinery already existed — PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 password hashes in an XOR-obfuscated `/etc/passwd`, with `auth_add_user`/`auth_verify` reading/writing it via raw ext2 — but it never engaged: `auth_setup`'s guard `if (block_size==0 || !ext2_resolve(AUTH_PATH))` conflated "no disk" with "no passwd file yet", so on a fresh ext2 disk it fell back to 3 hardcoded **in-memory** users and the "create default `/etc/passwd`" code right below it was **unreachable dead code** — nothing was ever persisted (and it wrongly printed "EXT2 not available" when ext2 was in fact mounted). Fixes (`kernel/auth.c`): the guard is split into three cases — no disk → in-memory fallback; disk **with** an existing passwd → use it; disk **without** one → create the default `nyx` account **and write it to disk** (now reachable). `write_passwd` also `ext2_mkdir("/etc")` first, since a freshly `mkfs`'d disk has only `lost+found` and `ext2_create_file` needs the parent dir to exist. `auth_add_user` gained a duplicate-username guard, and a new `auth_list_users` backs a new **`users`** shell command; **`useradd <user> <pass>`** persists an account via `auth_add_user`. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU with a two-boot persistence test on one ext2 image: boot 1 (fresh disk) logs `Created default user 'nyx' at /etc/passwd (persistent…)`, logs in as nyx, `useradd alice alicepw`, `users` → `nyx`+`alice`; **boot 2 on the same disk** logs `Persistent user file found at /etc/passwd`, and **login as alice/alicepw succeeds** (`[LOGIN] OK.`) with `users` → `nyx`+`alice` — the account survived the reboot. |
| v5.8.65 | **Account security hardening — random per-user salts, a stronger KDF, a password policy, and login lockout.** Four fixes to the auth stack: **(1) Random per-account salt.** The salt used to be *derived deterministically* from the username via `HMAC(SALT_SECRET, username)` with `SALT_SECRET` a compile-time constant — so the same username+password hashed **identically on every NyxOS install** (precomputable / rainbow-table-able, and it revealed duplicate passwords). `gen_salt_hex` is replaced by `gen_random_salt` (`kernel/auth.c`), which mixes RDTSC (CPU cycle counter), the 1000 Hz tick, and the RTC clock through splitmix64 into a random 8-byte salt; the salt is stored per `/etc/passwd` entry (the verifier already reads it back, so the change is transparent and backward-compatible). **(2) Stronger KDF:** `PBKDF2_ITERATIONS` 1000 → 10000 (stored per entry, so old hashes still verify). **(3) Password policy:** `auth_add_user`/`useradd` reject an empty username or a password under `AUTH_MIN_PASS` (4) characters. **(4) Login lockout + throttle:** `login_screen` (`kernel/login.c`) used to `return 0` (→ reboot) on the *first* wrong password; it now **retries in a loop** with a delay that grows after each failure and **locks out (reboots) after 3 attempts** — real brute-force friction with a usable retry UX. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU: (a) **random salt** — two *fresh* disks each auto-create `nyx`/`nyx`, yet their `/etc/passwd` entries differ (`nyx:7ea70b…:10000:1d4ab9…` vs `nyx:dc91ca…:10000:ebd84c…`) — same credentials, different salt+hash (deterministic salting would have made them identical), both at 10000 iterations; (b) **functional** — a two-boot run: `useradd x ab` is rejected (`password too short (minimum 4 characters)`), `useradd bob bobpass` persists, and after reboot a **wrong login is retried** (`[LOGIN] FAIL.` then a fresh prompt) before `bob/bobpass` logs in `[LOGIN] OK.` — proving the retry loop, persistence, and correct random-salt read-back. |
| v5.8.66 | **Create your own account from the login screen, with a profile picture.** On first use `nyx`/`nyx` is still the built-in *guest*, but the login screen can now **register a new account**: press **TAB** to switch from Login to a "Create your account" panel — enter a username + password (subject to the v5.8.65 policy) and pick **one of four profile pictures** with keys 1–4, then Enter creates the account (persisted to `/etc/passwd`, PBKDF2-salted) and logs straight in. The four avatars are simple procedural pixel-art tiles — **Moon** (purple), **Smile** (blue), **Cool** (green visor), **Cat** (orange, with ears) — drawn by a new shared `draw_avatar(x,y,size,id,selected)` in `kernel/login.c` (any size, so it also renders tiny in the taskbar). The chosen avatar id is stored as a **5th field** on the passwd entry (`user:salt:iter:hash:avatar`; `parse_entry` stops at the hash and ignores it, so old 4-field entries still verify) via `format_entry`/`auth_add_user(user,pass,avatar)`/new `auth_get_avatar(user)` (`kernel/auth.c`); `useradd <user> <pass> [avatar]` gained the optional id too. `login_screen` was reworked into a full login-vs-create panel (`draw_panel`) and now records the account that logged in in globals `g_login_user`/`g_login_avatar`; the desktop **taskbar draws that user's avatar + name** as a "current user" badge beside the clock (`kernel/compositor.c`, window buttons reserve room for it). **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU (screenshots): the create panel shows the 4 avatars with the picked one ringed; creating `maya` (avatar "Cool") logs in and the taskbar shows `maya` + the green badge; after a reboot, logging back in as `maya` shows the **same** avatar — read back from the persisted `/etc/passwd`. |
| v5.8.67 | **Login fixes — a failed login no longer closes the VM, and the create-account panel no longer overflows.** Two bugs reported against v5.8.66: **(1) QEMU closed when creating an account.** Root cause: the v5.8.65 login lockout did `return 0` after 3 failed attempts, and the boot caller reacts to that by **rebooting** — which under QEMU's `-no-reboot` (used by `run.ps1`) just *exits the VM*. A user who typed new credentials while still in **login** mode (TAB not pressed, or pressed twice) would fail 3 times and the window would vanish. Fixed in `kernel/login.c`: the lockout now shows "locked briefly", waits out a **cooldown, resets the counter, and keeps the login screen up** instead of ever returning 0 — brute-force is still throttled, but the VM never closes and the user can retry or press TAB to sign up. **(2) Misaligned create panel.** The login-mode hint line was ~48 chars, wider than the 380px panel, so it ran off the right edge and left a stray `nt.` fragment when toggling to create mode; it's now a shorter, centered string that fits, and every label/message in the panel was checked to stay within bounds. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU headless (no disk, like `run.ps1`): 3 wrong logins now log `[LOGIN] LOCKED (cooldown).` (not "rebooting"), **QEMU stays alive** through the cooldown, and creating an account afterward still logs in to the desktop; the create panel screenshot is clean with no overflow artifact. |
| v5.8.68 | **Login create-panel crash fix — repaint only the active field per keystroke, not the whole panel.** A user reported QEMU closing *while typing* in the "Create your account" panel (real SDL display + keyboard; not reproducible headless even with rapid/Spanish-layout/mouse input). Root cause: the v5.8.66 login rewrite repainted the **entire panel** — box, all four avatars (`draw_avatar` ×4), every label and both fields — **on every keystroke**, straight to the linear framebuffer with no double-buffering. That ~15× heavier per-keystroke redraw hammered the LFB and destabilised the login on real hardware (a diagnostic build's extra per-key `serial_puts` masked it by adding delay — the tell that it was timing-sensitive). Fix (`kernel/login.c`): a new `draw_active_field()` repaints just the field being typed into (as the pre-rewrite login did); `draw_panel()` full repaints are kept only for the events that actually change the rest of the panel — mode toggle (TAB), field advance (Enter), and avatar selection (1-4). **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified headless that typing shows the text in the field, the avatars stay drawn, and account creation still logs in to the desktop; the crash itself is environment-specific so final confirmation is on the reporter's machine. |
| v5.8.69 | **Taskbar user menu — click your badge to change your profile picture or log out (back to the login, no reboot).** The logged-in user's avatar+name badge (added v5.8.66) is now clickable: it opens a small popup (`draw_user_menu`/`badge_hit`/`user_menu_*` in `kernel/compositor.c`) headed by the current avatar+name with two items. **Change picture** cycles to the next of the 4 avatars, updates the badge live, and **persists** it via new `auth_set_avatar(user, avatar)` (`kernel/auth.c`) — which rewrites just the 5th (avatar) field of that user's `/etc/passwd` entry, keeping the salt+hash (or updates the in-memory fallback with no disk). **Log out** sets a new exported flag `compositor_logout_requested` + `quit`, so `compositor_run()` returns; the boot flow (`kernel/kernel.c`) is now a **login→desktop loop** instead of a one-shot: on logout it re-shows the login screen without rebooting (`fb_use_lfb_direct()` first so the login draws to the LFB; `compositor_init()` now frees the previous session's windows so the desktop starts clean), and only a real Shutdown leaves the loop to reboot. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU (no disk, screenshots): logged in as nyx, clicked the badge → menu with the nyx header + "Change picture"/"Log out"; Change picture flipped the badge from the Moon to the Smile avatar; Log out returned to the NyxOS Login screen (QEMU stays alive — not a reboot) and logging back in reached a fresh desktop. |
| v5.8.70 | **Per-user home directories — the Terminal and File Manager start in `/home/<user>`.** On a successful login (or account creation), `login_screen` now calls `setup_user_home()` (`kernel/login.c`): it `vfs_mkdir`s `/home` and `/home/<user>` (both idempotent) and records the path in a new global `g_login_home`. New Terminal windows start there — `terminal_create_ctx` uses the new `login_home_node()` (which resolves `g_login_home` to its VFS node via the new public `vfs_path_node()` in `kernel/vfs.c`, or falls back to root) instead of `vfs_root_node()`, so the prompt reads `nyx:/home/nyx$`. The File Manager opens there too — `fileman_create_ctx` seeds its `cwd` from `g_login_home`. Because `g_login_home` is refreshed on every login, this composes with v5.8.69 logout: log out and back in as a different user and the new windows open in *that* user's home. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU (no disk, screenshots): logged in as nyx → the desktop Terminal prompt is `nyx:/home/nyx$` and opening the File Manager shows the path bar `/home/nyx`. |
| v5.8.71 | **Starter content for a new home directory.** The `/home/<user>` created at login (v5.8.70) is no longer empty: the first login that creates it (`setup_user_home` now checks `vfs_mkdir`'s return — 0 means freshly created — and calls a new `populate_home()` in `kernel/login.c`) seeds it with a `Documents/` and `Downloads/` folder and a `welcome.txt` note (greeting the user, pointing out double-click-to-edit and the taskbar user badge). It only runs on creation, so a later login (e.g. logout→relogin) never clobbers files the user changed. **No new syscalls** (still 44); `kernel/login.c` only. Verified in QEMU (no disk, screenshot): logging in as nyx and opening the File Manager (which starts at `/home/nyx`) shows "3 entries" — `Documents/`, `Downloads/`, `welcome.txt`. |
| v5.8.72 | **Minesweeper — a GUI game (new app) + a right-click routing fix.** A classic 9×9 / 10-mine Minesweeper as a compositor window app (`kernel/minesweeper_win.{c,h}`), launchable from the Start menu ("Minesweeper", item 12) and a new "Mines" desktop icon (action 12). Left-click uncovers a cell — mines are placed **lazily on the first click**, keeping the opening move *and its 8 neighbours* clear so the first reveal always flood-fills a comfortable opening (iterative stack-based flood, never recursion-deep); right-click plants/removes a flag; left-clicking an already-uncovered number **chords** (uncovers its hidden neighbours once the matching number of flags surround it). The header has a red-LED mines-left counter, a clickable **face button** that starts a new game (also `r`/`n`), and a live seconds **timer** (advances off the compositor's ~1 Hz clock repaint). Classic per-number colours (1 blue, 2 green, 3 red…), drawn flag/mine glyphs, and a smiley/cool/dead face for playing/won/lost. Geometry mirrors the draw path in the click handler using `win->y + TITLE_H` (the *correct* client origin), so hit-testing lines up exactly. **Also fixed a pre-existing compositor bug:** the right-click dispatch iterated the window array in **slot order** and delivered the click to whichever window sat earliest in the array, so an overlapped right-click leaked to a **lower** window — it now sorts **topmost-first by z-order** like the left-click path (this also repairs the File Manager context menu when it overlaps another window). Wiring in `kernel/compositor.c` + `kernel/Makefile`. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU (no disk, screenshots): opened from the Mines icon → fresh board (010 / smiley / 000); a centre click flood-filled an area showing blue `1` / green `2` numbers and the timer ticked to `001`; right-clicking hidden cells planted red flags and dropped the counter to `008`; the face button reset to a clean board. |
| v5.8.73 | **`wget` — a userspace HTTP client over the TCP sockets, with its own DNS-over-UDP resolver.** New `user/wget.c` (ships in the initramfs as `/wget.elf`): `wget [-O file] [-r resolver] [-p port] [-q] [-S] http://host[:port][/path]` (also a positional `host [port] [path]` form). It opens a `SOCK_STREAM` socket, `connect()`s, sends an HTTP/1.0 `GET` (with `Host:` + `Connection: close`), reads the whole response, splits headers/body at the blank line, parses the status code, and writes the body to stdout (or `-O <file>`); a status line (`wget: HTTP <code>, <n> bytes`) goes to stderr. Hostnames are resolved **entirely in userspace** — a self-contained A-record DNS query built and parsed over the existing `sendto()`/`recvfrom()` UDP calls to a resolver (default 10.0.2.3 = QEMU SLIRP's DNS; overridable with `-r`/`-p`), handling label QNAMEs and 0xC0 compression pointers in the answer; dotted-quad hosts skip DNS. **No new syscalls** (still 44) and **no kernel changes** — a pure userspace program on the existing socket API. Run it from the userspace shell (`exec /sh.elf` → `wget …`), since the kernel shell's `exec` passes only the path (no argv). Wiring: `kernel/Makefile` (USER_ELFS + build rules). Verified in QEMU with SLIRP networking (`-netdev user -device rtl8139`) against a local HTTP server + a local DNS responder reached via the SLIRP host alias 10.0.2.2 (screenshots): after `layout us` + `dhcp`, from `sh.elf` — (1) `wget http://10.0.2.2:8000/hello.txt` printed the file body and `wget: HTTP 200, 35 bytes`; (2) `wget -r 10.0.2.2 -p 5354 http://nyxhost:8000/hello.txt` logged `resolving nyxhost …`, resolved it to 10.0.2.2 via the userspace DNS query, then fetched the body. (De-risked first with the kernel's own `httpget`, which fetched the same file — proving userspace sockets egress the NIC via SLIRP.) |
| v5.8.74 | **`exec` forwards argv — userspace tools run with arguments straight from the kernel shell.** Previously the GUI-terminal shell's `exec <file>` called `spawn_user_path(path)` and passed **only the path**, so anything needing arguments (wget, grep, cat…) had to be run from the userspace shell (`exec /sh.elf` → `cmd args`). Now `exec <file> a b c` (and `spawn`) build the child's SysV argv frame and launch it directly. New `build_argv_stack(pd, stack_top, argv, argc)` (`kernel/process.c`) is the spawn-side counterpart to do_execve's frame builder: it points the global `user_cr3` at the **new** process's page directory and `copy_to_user`s the strings + the `[argc, argv…, NULL, envp NULL]` qword frame onto its fresh stack, returning the adjusted ring-3 RSP — with **interrupts masked across the copy** (unlike do_execve, which already runs inside a masked syscall, this path runs from the shell with IF=1, so a scheduler tick must not swap `user_cr3` mid-loop). New `elf_load_args()` (`kernel/elf.c`) = `elf_load_image` + `build_argv_stack` + `create_user_process(entry, rsp, pd)`; `elf_load` becomes a thin `argc==0` wrapper (byte-identical no-arg behavior). New `spawn_user_path_args(path, argv, argc)` (`kernel/kernel.c`) mirrors `spawn_user_path` (now a wrapper) and records the space-joined argv as the process cmdline; `cmd_exec` / `cmd_spawn` forward `&argv[1], argc-1`. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU (screenshots): from the GUI Terminal, `exec /echo.elf NyxOS exec argv works` printed `NyxOS exec argv works`, and (after `dhcp`) `exec /wget.elf http://10.0.2.2:8000/hello.txt` fetched the file and printed `HTTP 200, 35 bytes` — both exiting code 0, with no `/sh.elf` step. |
| v5.8.75 | **The kernel shell auto-execs userspace ELFs — run tools by bare name, no `exec` prefix.** When a typed command isn't a builtin, `execute_command` (the GUI-terminal shell) now resolves it to a userspace ELF and runs it with argv instead of printing "Command not found". New `resolve_user_elf(name, out, sz)` (`kernel/kernel.c`): a name containing `/` is used verbatim, otherwise `/<name>.elf` then `/<name>` are probed in the initramfs (first that opens wins). New `run_foreground_elf(path, argv, argc)` factors the spawn + foreground-wait (repaint-while-running) + reap + exit-code logic out of `cmd_exec` (now a one-line wrapper) and is shared with the fallback. Kernel builtins keep priority — they're matched first, so `ls`/`cat`/`grep` keep their built-in behavior and only unknown names hit auto-exec. Builds directly on v5.8.74's argv forwarding. **No new syscalls** (still 44); only `kernel/kernel.c`. Verified in QEMU (screenshot): typed with no `exec`, `whoami` printed `nyx` (auto-exec of `/whoami.elf`), and `wget http://10.0.2.2:8000/hello.txt` fetched the file and printed `wget: HTTP 200, 35 bytes` — both reporting `[exec] PID N exited (code 0)`. |
| v5.8.76 | **Tab-completion now includes userspace ELFs.** The GUI-terminal shell's command-word Tab-completion (`command_complete` / `command_list_matches`) previously knew only builtins; now that a bare `wget`/`whoami` auto-execs (v5.8.75), completion also offers the base names (minus `.elf`) of programs in the initramfs root. New `enum_completions(cb, ctx)` (`kernel/kernel.c`) walks the builtins then `readdir("/")`'s `*.elf` entries, skipping any whose base name shadows a builtin (`is_builtin()`) so each name is offered once; both completion functions were refactored onto it with small ctx callbacks (list = space-joined matches, complete = single match or common prefix). **No new syscalls** (still 44); only `kernel/kernel.c`. Verified in QEMU (screenshots): typing `whoa`+Tab completed to `whoami` and ran it (printed `nyx`) — an ELF-name completion — while `dat`+Tab still completed to the `date` builtin (no regression). (Note: headless `sendkey tab` injection is itself unreliable — some runs drop the keystroke entirely — but completion is correct whenever Tab is actually delivered; a real keypress is reliable.) |
| v5.8.77 | **wget follows redirects + an `open` command = download-and-read.** Two halves of a "fetch a page and view it" loop. (1) **wget 3xx redirects** (`user/wget.c`): the fetch is now a loop over (host, port, path) that, on a 301/302/303/307/308 carrying a `Location:` header, rewrites the target and re-requests — up to 5 hops. `find_location()` scans the header block case-insensitively; the Location may be an absolute `http://host:port/path` (switches host) or root-relative `/path` (same host/port). (2) **`open <file>`** — a new kernel-shell builtin (`cmd_open` in `kernel/kernel.c`) that opens a file in the GUI Text Editor via the existing `compositor_open_editor()`; it resolves the path to absolute against the shell cwd and checks the file exists first. Safe because the kernel shell is dispatched on the compositor thread (which owns the window list) — a userspace program can't create a window, so this belongs in the kernel. **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU (screenshots): `wget http://10.0.2.2:8000/old` logged `redirect -> /hello.txt`, re-fetched, and printed the target body (`HTTP 200, 41 bytes`); then `wget -O /tmp/page.txt http://…/hello.txt` saved it and `open /tmp/page.txt` popped a Text Editor window showing the downloaded text (`Opened: /tmp/page.txt (41 bytes)`). Both ran as bare commands (auto-exec + the new builtin). |
| v5.8.78 | **Concurrent multi-process sockets: hardening + a repro — the "sockets stay single-process" garble no longer reproduces.** New `user/netstorm.c` forks 4 processes that each hammer the loopback echo (25 write/read rounds on their own connection); before this it failed reliably (~1 of 4 processes' `connect()` hung in `SYN_SENT` — its SYN-ACK lost under concurrent loopback handshakes). Added net-stack concurrency hardening, each part addressing a genuine hazard: **(1) re-entrancy serialization** — `kernel_poll_net()` and the `nsock_*` ops (connect/send/recv/accept/listen/close) run under `preempt_disable()`, so while one process drives the *non-reentrant* loopback-ring drain + TCP state machine, `irq_scheduler_tick` won't switch to another process mid-flight (IRQs stay on; blocking calls release preemption during their idle delay so peers still progress); **(2) atomic ephemeral-port pick** — the client source port used a non-atomic `ephemeral++`, so two concurrent connects could grab the SAME port (then the second's SYN matched the first's server conn instead of spawning its own → no SYN-ACK); now it's inside the `preempt_disable` window; **(3) connection-table capacity** — `TCP_MAX_CONNS` 8→32 and `ECHO_MAX` 8→16 (N concurrent loopback clients need 1+2N slots; 8 overflowed at 4). **No new syscalls** (still 44). Verified in QEMU: netstorm passed **38 consecutive fresh-boot runs** where it previously failed ~1/4. **Correction (see v5.8.79):** what looked like an "ultra-fragile timing Heisenbug" was actually a **conn-table capacity limit** (`TCP_MAX_CONNS` 8 < the 1+2N slots N concurrent clients need) **masked by a Makefile stale-build gap** — the `TCP_MAX_CONNS` bump in tcp.h wasn't recompiling `tcp.o`, so it silently didn't take effect, and edits that touched tcp.c only *seemed* to "fix by perturbation." Root-caused deterministically under `-icount` in v5.8.79; the capacity bump (32) is the robust fix. `netstorm` ships as the regression repro. |
| v5.8.79 | **Root-caused the v5.8.78 concurrent-socket "Heisenbug": it was a conn-table capacity limit masked by a Makefile stale-build gap — and fixed the Makefile.** Reproduced netstorm's failure **deterministically under QEMU `-icount`** (virtual time makes the timing race reproducible AND stable under observation, unlike host-time where printf/tracing "perturbed it away"), then dumped the conn table at the stuck `connect()`: all 8 `conns[]` slots were full — 1 listener + 3 fully-established client+server pairs + the 4th client's `SYN_SENT` — so the 4th client's SYN found `find_slot()` empty → **no server child → no SYN-ACK → SYN_SENT hang**. The real fix is **`TCP_MAX_CONNS` 8→32** (v5.8.78 already bumped it): N concurrent loopback clients to one service need 1+2N slots. Why it looked like a fragile Heisenbug: the **Makefile only listed `kernel.h` as a header prereq** (`%.o: %.c kernel.h`), not `tcp.h`, so bumping `TCP_MAX_CONNS` did **not** recompile `tcp.o`/`net.o` — the 8-slot table stayed compiled in until an unrelated `tcp.c` edit forced a rebuild that finally applied the 32 (so edits that touched tcp.c seemed to "fix by perturbation"). **Fix:** GCC auto-dependencies — `-MMD -MP` + `-include $(OBJS_KERNEL:.o=.d)`, so every object depends on every header it `#include`s; `clean` also removes `*.d`. Verified: clean build passes netstorm; editing tcp.h now rebuilds tcp.o/net.o (timestamps + behaviour). The v5.8.78 serialization/atomic-port hardening stay (correct; they shift timing so 8 *mostly* passes — 5/6 — but 32 is the robust fix). **No new syscalls.** |
| v5.8.80 | **Four POSIX file syscalls — `stat`/`fstat`/`lseek`/`getppid` (SYS 44–47) — plus two Makefile build-correctness fixes that had been silently shipping stale kernels.** (1) **Syscalls:** `SYS_STAT`(44)/`SYS_FSTAT`(45) fill a userspace `struct stat` (`st_size`, `st_mode` with S_IFDIR/S_IFREG, `st_ino`) via new `vfs_stat(path,…)`/`vfs_fstat(fd,…)` (dir → mode `0x41ED`, regular → `0x81A4`); `SYS_LSEEK`(46) moves the per-fd offset (`ufd_offset_of`) with SEEK_SET/CUR/END (END uses `vfs_fsize`), rejecting pipes/sockets; `SYS_GETPPID`(47) returns `cur->ppid`. `user/syscall.h` gains `struct stat`, the `S_IS*`/`SEEK_*` macros and four wrappers; new `user/stat.c` → `/stat.elf` (`stat <path>`) exercises all four. Syscall count 44→48.
