(2) **Build fixes (continuing the v5.8.79 staleness saga):** the *real* reason incremental builds shipped stale kernels was **`-include $(…d)` sitting ABOVE the `all:` target** — the first auto-dep `.d` (kernel.o's) hijacked make's **default goal to `kernel.o`**, so a bare `make` (and `build.ps1`, which runs `make -C kernel` with no target) built only kernel.o and never relinked `nyx-kernel.bin`; only `make clean` (which deletes the `.d`s → `all` becomes first again) had been restoring correct builds. Fixed with an explicit **`.DEFAULT_GOAL := all`**. Second, the generated **`initramfs_data.h` now regenerates via a `FORCE` + content-compare** recipe (always re-runs mkinitramfs into a temp, `mv`s over the header only when the bytes differ) so a freshly-built `user/*.elf` can never be missed, yet unchanged builds don't needlessly relink — this is what had let `/stat.elf` be absent from the initramfs (`Loaded 53` of 54) even though it sat on disk. Verified in QEMU: `stat /sh.elf` → `size=23480 type=file mode=0x81a4`, `fstat` matches, `lseek(mid)` and `lseek(SEEK_END)` correct, `getppid` → the shell's pid; `stat /` → `type=dir`; boot now loads all **54** initramfs files. |
| v5.8.81 | **VFS hardening — closed a latent heap OOB and raised the capacity caps.** `vfs_node_t` holds a fixed `children[MAX_CHILDREN]` array, and **eight creation paths appended with an unchecked `children[child_count++] = node`** — once a directory hit `MAX_CHILDREN` the next insert wrote one element past the array, corrupting the adjacent inode in the static `nodes[]` pool. Not hypothetical: `/` already holds 54 initramfs files + the mount dirs (~58 children) and the old cap was **64**. Fix: **(1)** a single `vfs_append_child(parent, child)` choke point returns -1 at capacity (callers `free_node()` the just-alloc'd node and return their own error) and now backs every insert in `vfs_open`(O_CREAT), `vfs_create_from_mem`, `vfs_mkdir`, `vfs_rename`, `vfs_chdir`, `vfs_touch`, `vfs_write_file`, `vfs_cp` — `vfs_rename` appends to the destination *before* unlinking from the source, so a full target can't orphan the node; the two already-guarded paths (`proc_make`, mount-stub) keep their pre-`alloc` checks. **(2)** Caps raised: `MAX_INODES` 128→256, `MAX_CHILDREN` 64→128 (root now has 2× headroom; `nodes[]` BSS ~91KB→~313KB — fine at 256 MB RAM). **(3)** The initramfs loader (`kernel/initramfs.c`) now **logs a warning** instead of silently dropping a file when the VFS is full, and `file_count` reflects what actually loaded. New `user/vfsfill.c` → `/vfsfill.elf` regression test fills a dir until `open()` refuses, then confirms an unrelated inode still stats + cleans up. **No new syscalls** (still 48). Verified in QEMU: boot loads all **55** initramfs files with **0** VFS-full warnings; `vfsfill` created exactly **128** files then `open()` returned <0 ("refused cleanly, no crash"), `/sh.elf` still stat'd (no corruption), 128 files cleaned up, and `stat /tmp` worked afterward — system fully alive. |
| v5.8.82 | **`dup` + `rename` syscalls, and shell-spawned tools now inherit the terminal's cwd.** Investigating "make cwd per-process" surfaced that it *already is*: the process struct carries `cwd[MAX_PATH]`, `do_fork` copies the parent's, `copy_path_from_user` routes every relative path through `path_resolve(cur->cwd, …)` (which handles `.`/`..`/`//`), and `chdir`/`getcwd` (SYS 21/22) read/write it. The real gap: `create_user_process` `memset`s the struct, so a **spawned** (non-forked) tool started at `/` regardless of the terminal's `cd`. Fixes: **(1)** `create_user_process` now seeds `p->cwd` from the spawning context's cwd (`vfs_getcwd()` — the global `current_dir` the compositor sets per-terminal), so a tool run after `cd /tmp` begins in `/tmp`. **(2)** New **`SYS_DUP`(48)** — `dup(oldfd)` returns the lowest-available ufd aliasing oldfd (pipe ends `pipe_incref`; VFS/socket handles alias — safe because `vfs_close` is a no-op for the ramdisk nodes ring 3 opens), and both fds stay open, unlike `dup2`'s move. **(3)** New **`SYS_RENAME`(49)** — `rename(old,new)` wraps the v5.8.81-hardened `vfs_rename`, refusing a missing source and confirming the destination landed. Userspace `dup`/`rename` wrappers added; new `user/fsx.c`→`/fsx.elf` demo. Syscall count 48→50. Verified in QEMU: a bare `fsx` printed `start cwd = /home/nyx` (inherited the Terminal's home, not `/`); after `cd /tmp` a second `fsx` printed `start cwd = /tmp` (inherited the `cd`); `rename a->b = 0` (old gone, new present); `dup(3)=4` still read the file after `close(3)`; relative `stat("fsx_b")` resolved under `/tmp` and `chdir("..")` walked to `/`. |
| v5.8.83 | **THE pipeline-corruption Heisenbug — root-caused and FIXED after spanning many increments.** For releases the shared-libc pipeline/fork churn (`ls\|less`, `pstorm`) intermittently corrupted memory, surfacing as a `heap_free` #GP, a `pdpte=0xFFFF…FF` page-table MAPCORRUPT, or a userspace SIGSEGV — the same corruption wearing different masks, and famously "un-pin-able" (every `printf`/instrumentation change moved which mask fired first). **Root cause:** `init_memory` reserved only page 0 and `[0x100000, _kernel_end)`, leaving pages **`0x1000–0xFFFFF` marked FREE** — including the legacy firmware hole **`0xA0000–0xFFFFF`** (the VGA framebuffer MMIO window and the video/system **BIOS ROMs**, which QEMU/SeaBIOS shadow write-protected). We never parse the multiboot RAM map, so `alloc_page` (which scans the bitmap low→high) handed those non-RAM frames out **under memory pressure** — writes are dropped, reads return firmware bytes — and the allocator laid live kernel structures (heap/slab metadata, page tables, user pages) onto ROM, then read back BIOS garbage. That is the entire Heisenbug: **stress-dependent** (the low RAM pool must be exhausted to climb into the `0xA0000+` hole, so light use never reaches it) and **manifestation-varying** (which mask fires depends on what the ROM frame was reused for). **How it was finally caught (perturbation-proof):** QEMU **`-icount shift=3`** (deterministic virtual time — makes the race reproducible on run 1 *and* observation-stable) + a native-gdb **hardware watchpoint set from boot** on the corrupt slot (`0xc2fc0`), conditioned on the exact bad value — it stopped with **`cr3=0`, `pc` inside SeaBIOS's high self-relocation**, i.e. the "writer" was the *firmware at POST*: the value was ROM content that had sat there since boot and was never RAM. Trail: `heap_free+22` #GP on a non-canonical `block->next` → the block lived at `0xc2fc0`, which is inside the `0xC0000–0xC7FFF` video-BIOS window → `alloc_page` had handed out a ROM frame. **Fix (one line):** `init_memory` now reserves all of **`[0, _kernel_end)`** (loop from page 0, not `0x100000/PAGE_SIZE`). Safe — the kernel loads at 1 MB and never allocates sub-1 MB RAM; the SMP trampoline at `0x8000` is a direct `memcpy` (now *protected* from `alloc_page`, not clobberable), and VGA/BIOS MMIO is accessed directly, never via the allocator; the ~640 KB of conventional RAM below 1 MB is a negligible forgone amount. **Also fixed a second, independent unreserved region** (a secondary path to the identical corruption, reached only after both the low pool and the low hole are exhausted): with `-mcmodel=large`, GCC emits large objects into **`.lbss`/`.ldata`/`.lrodata`**, which the linker script's `.bss`/`.data`/`.rodata` did **not** capture — so `page_refcount[]`, `page_pinned[]`, `pipes[]`, `nodes[]`, and `libc_segs[]` landed **past `_kernel_end`**, also unreserved. `linker.ld` now folds `.lbss/.ldata/.lrodata` into `.bss/.data/.rodata` (all five arrays confirmed below `_kernel_end` by `nm`). Plus a build fix: **`nyx-kernel.bin` now depends on `linker.ld`** — a linker-script-only edit was silently not relinking on incremental builds (the same stale-build class as the earlier tcp.h/`.DEFAULT_GOAL` bugs), which briefly masked the `.lbss` fix as ineffective. **No new syscalls** (still 50). **Verified:** `pstorm 60` × 4 real-time = **4/4 CLEAN** (0 faults / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC; was **0/4** before), and `pstorm 50` under **`-icount`** = **CLEAN** — the exact deterministic mode that had failed on run 1. |
| v5.8.84 | **Physical allocator now honors the multiboot2 memory map — the principled generalization of the v5.8.83 firmware-hole fix.** v5.8.83 stopped `alloc_page` from handing out the *low* firmware hole by hardcoding a reserve of `[0, _kernel_end)`, but that's blind to reserved regions *above* the kernel (safe only because `pstorm` never allocated high enough to reach them). Now `kernel_main` parses the **multiboot2 type-6 memory-map tag** into an `mb_mmap_entry_t[]` (kernel.h) and `init_memory(mem_size, mmap, count)` does the canonical thing: **mark every page used, then free ONLY the ranges the firmware reports as available RAM (type 1)**, and finally carve the kernel image + the whole low 1 MB back out (page 0 / real-mode IVT-BDA / the SMP trampoline at 0x8000). Every hole is now reserved automatically, whatever the RAM size or layout. Verified against QEMU's actual map for `-m 256M` — 7 regions incl. **`0xffe0000 len=0x20000 type=2`, a 128 KB firmware-reserved block near the top of RAM that v5.8.83 would have freed** (the exact class of high hole that could have reawakened the corruption under heavier load), plus the low EBDA (`0x9fc00`)/BIOS-ROM (`0xf0000`) ranges and the high BIOS-flash (`0xfffc0000`)/PCI-MMIO (`0xfd00000000`) holes. When no type-6 tag is present it falls back to the exact v5.8.83 behavior (reserve `[0,_kernel_end)`, free the rest). Boot now prints `[MEM] mmap 7 regions: 60699 pages free (237 MB usable), RAM top 255 MB`. **No new syscalls** (still 50). Verified: system boots + runs normally and `pstorm 40` is **CLEAN** (0 faults / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC). |
| v5.8.85 | **User stacks are now demand-grown with a guard page (was a fixed, pre-committed 64 KB).** Every user process used to get exactly `USER_STACK_PAGES`=16 pages (64 KB) mapped up front at exec — a hard ceiling (a deep call chain #PF'd → SIGSEGV at 64 KB) that also committed 64 KB of RAM per process even for tiny programs. Now `elf_load_image` / `create_user_process` commit only **`USER_STACK_INIT_PAGES`=4 (16 KB)**, and **`vm_handle_fault` grows the stack on demand**: a USER not-present fault in `[USER_STACK_LOW, USER_STACK_TOP)` below the committed pages materialises a fresh zeroed RW+NX page, down to a floor of **`USER_STACK_MAX_PAGES`=128 (512 KB)**. A fault below the floor is a genuine overflow → falls through to SIGSEGV; the page just under the floor `[USER_STACK_LOW-PAGE_SIZE, USER_STACK_LOW)` is a permanent unmapped **guard page**, and a fault there prints `[stack overflow] pid=… comm=… (cr2=…)` for a clear diagnostic before the kill. So a deep program now gets up to 512 KB (8× the old limit) while a plain one commits ~16 KB, and a runaway recursion is caught cleanly instead of dying at an unlabeled #PF. Placed before the `pte_ptr` walk (a grown page has no PTE yet); fork's COW and execve's fresh image both work unchanged (the constants are fixed VAs, no per-process state). New `kernel.h` constants (`USER_STACK_TOP`/`INIT_PAGES`/`MAX_PAGES`/`LOW`); `elf.c` + `process.c` stack setup loop to `USER_STACK_INIT_PAGES`; `paging.c` grow+guard blocks. New `user/stacktest.c` → `/stacktest.elf` (non-tail recursion, ~4 KB/frame). **No new syscalls** (still 50). Verified in QEMU: `stacktest` recurses ~192 KB and **SURVIVES** (was fatal at 64 KB); `stacktest 300` (~1.2 MB) trips the guard — kernel prints `[stack overflow]` and the process dies with SIGSEGV; `pstorm 40` stays **CLEAN** (0 faults / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC — the fault-handler change is a clean regression). |
| v5.8.86 | **SMEP + SMAP — hardware-enforced ring-0 ↔ user isolation.** Enable the CPU's Supervisor Mode Execution/Access Prevention when the processor advertises them (CPUID.(EAX=7,ECX=0):EBX bit 7 = SMEP, bit 20 = SMAP): **CR4.SMEP** (bit 20) makes ring 0 **executing** a user (U=1) page a #GP; **CR4.SMAP** (bit 21) makes ring 0 **reading/writing** a user page a #GP unless RFLAGS.AC is set (stac/clac). The enabling insight: **NyxOS's kernel never touches user pages directly** — it runs on the kernel CR3 (user pages unmapped) and reaches user data only by walking the user tables to a PHYSICAL address (`user_v2p`) and accessing it through the identity map, a supervisor page. Every user-memory access already funnels through `copy_to_user`/`copy_from_user`/`copy_str_from_user` (and every caller, incl. signal-frame delivery), so **no stac/clac bracketing is needed**, and turning SMEP/SMAP on becomes a **hardware-enforced audit** of that isolation invariant: any future stray user-VA access from ring 0 now traps instead of silently succeeding (it would also stop the classic "corrupt kernel pointer executes user-controlled code" escalation). New `enable_smep_smap()` (kernel.c): CPUID-gated CR4 set + `clac` (AC starts clear); called on the BSP after `init_paging`, and by each AP (smp.c — CR4 is per-CPU). Because it's CPUID-gated it's a safe no-op on CPUs lacking the features — including the default QEMU `qemu64`. Boot prints `[CPU] SMEP=on/unavailable SMAP=on/unavailable`. **No new syscalls** (still 50). Verified two ways: with **`-cpu qemu64,+smep,+smap`** the boot reports `SMEP=on SMAP=on` and `stacktest` + `pstorm 30` run with **0 faults / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC / 0 #GP** — the heavy fork/pipe/execve churn never once accessed a user page from ring 0, proving the invariant under hardware enforcement; with plain **`qemu64`** it reports `unavailable` and boots identically (graceful no-op). |
| v5.8.87 | **Real user threads — `clone(CLONE_VM)` + `futex` (SYS 50/51, count 50→52).** **`do_clone(fn, stack, arg, flags)`** creates a scheduled ring-3 task that **SHARES** the caller's address space (`t->page_directory = self->page_directory`) instead of taking fork's COW copy — that one line is the whole thread property. It gets its own kernel stack from new `init_thread_task_stack()`, which builds the iretq frame with RIP=`fn`, RSP=the caller-supplied stack and **RDI=`arg`** (the 6th push of the high→low `rax,rbx,rcx,rdx,rsi,rdi,rbp,r8..r15` SAVE_REGS block), so the thread enters as `fn(arg)` under the SysV ABI. Without `CLONE_VM` clone() is refused (fork remains the separate-address-space primitive). **Address-space lifetime** is the subtle part: several `process_t` now share one PML4, so a new **`addr_space_shared(pml4, except)`** guards *all four* `free_page_directory` sites (execve's `old_pd`, `reap_user_process`, `reap_zombies`, `do_waitpid`) — only the LAST task out releases the space, in any exit order. **`do_futex(uaddr, op, val)`**: `FUTEX_WAIT` blocks while `*uaddr == val` (returning at once if it already differs — the compare-and-sleep that makes a lock race-free), `FUTEX_WAKE` wakes up to `val` waiters. The queue is keyed by the word's **physical** address (`user_v2p`, de-static'd in syscall.c) so every task mapping the page agrees on the key, and the word is read through the **identity map** — the same supervisor path `copy_to_user` uses, so it stays SMEP/SMAP-clean (v5.8.86). WAIT parks with the `do_waitpid` discipline (`blocked_in_kernel`, and save/restore of the shared `user_cr3`/`user_rsp` across the `sti;hlt`); the compare and the park are atomic under the syscall's interrupt mask, so no wakeup is lost. Userspace gains `clone()`/`futex_wait()`/`futex_wake()` wrappers plus new `user/threads.c` → `/threads.elf`: 4 threads × 2000 increments of a shared counter behind a textbook futex mutex (atomic `xchg` fast path, syscall only on contention). **Known limits (honest):** `program_break`, the VMA table and fds are still per-`process_t` *copies* taken at clone time, so post-clone `sbrk`/`mmap`/fd changes don't propagate between threads (multi-threaded `malloc` isn't coherent yet), and a thread is given no `file_buf` ownership (file-backed mmap in a thread zero-fills) so a sibling's exit can't double-free it. Verified in QEMU: **4 tids started**, shared counter **= 8000 of 8000 → OK** (proving both the shared address space and the mutex — 8000 deliberately non-atomic increments serialized correctly), and `pstorm 30` still **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC** (the teardown guard is a clean regression). |
| v5.8.88 | **Thread coherence — one heap and one mmap table per thread group (multi-threaded `malloc` now works).** v5.8.87 shipped real threads but left `program_break`/`heap_start` and the VMA table as per-`process_t` **copies** taken at clone time: each thread bumped its *own* break, so two threads `malloc`ing were handed **overlapping** regions, and a page `sbrk`'d by one thread wasn't in a sibling's fault-in window (spurious SIGSEGV). Fixed by giving every task a **`tgid`** — the pid of the thread-group **leader** that owns the shared heap/VMA state (0 or own pid ⇒ it *is* the leader; `do_clone` sets `t->tgid = tg_leader(self)->pid`, so a thread cloning a thread still joins the one group). A pid rather than a pointer **by design**: an exited leader can never leave a dangling reference — `tg_leader()` resolves by `find_process()` and falls back to self. Six chokepoints now resolve through it: **`SYS_SBRK`**, the **heap window in `vm_handle_fault`** (a page sbrk'd by one thread must fault in for any sibling), and mmap.c's **`vma_find` / `do_mmap` / `do_mprotect` / `do_munmap`** — so allocation, lookup and teardown all hit one shared table and two threads can never be handed the same region. **`mmap_free_bufs` is deliberately NOT redirected**: those `file_buf` snapshots are per-task property, and redirecting it would let a thread's exit free the leader's buffers. Leader-exits-first is handled too: new **`tg_reassign_leader()`** (called from all three reap paths before `mmap_free_bufs`) hands the shared heap/VMA state — *including `file_buf` ownership*, zeroing it in the dying leader so it isn't freed out from under the heir — to a surviving member and re-points the rest at it, so the group stays coherent in any exit order. `user/threads.c` extended: each thread now `malloc`s and signs a block, and main verifies all blocks are **distinct, non-overlapping and intact** — exactly the check that would have failed before this. **No new syscalls** (still 52). **Remaining limit (honest):** the fd table is still a per-task copy taken at clone, so fds opened *after* the clone aren't shared between threads. Verified in QEMU: 4 tids, counter **8000/8000 → OK**, **heap → OK**, and `pstorm 30` still **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.8.89 | **Shared fd table per thread group — the thread model is now complete.** v5.8.88 gave threads one heap and one mmap table but left the descriptor table a per-task copy taken at clone, so an fd opened *after* the clone was invisible to siblings (and each thread's copy held its own pipe refcounts). Now the fd table follows the same `tg_leader()` rule. The redirect landed almost entirely in **four small helpers** — `ufd_alloc` / `ufd_lookup` / `ufd_offset_of` / `ufd_release` — which every fd syscall already funnels through, so a one-line `fd_owner()` in each made open/close/read/write/lseek/fstat/getdents/pipe/socket group-wide at a stroke; only `dup`/`dup2`, which touch `p->ufd_*` directly, needed their own binding redirected. **Deliberately NOT redirected: `get_cur_proc()` in general** — `getpid()` and friends must still answer for the calling THREAD, so only fd paths resolve to the leader. `do_clone` now copies **no** fds at all (and takes no `pipe_incref`): a thread's own table stays empty, which is exactly what makes `close_proc_fds()` at its exit a safe no-op that leaves the group's descriptors open for its siblings — the same ownership discipline `mmap_free_bufs` uses. `tg_reassign_leader()` also hands the fd table to the heir and **clears it in the dying leader**, so a leader exiting first can't close descriptors the survivors are still using. `user/threads.c` extended: thread 0 opens `/thrfd.txt`, writes to it and leaves it open; **main then lseeks and reads back through that same fd** — a descriptor opened by a thread, used by another task, which simply would not exist with per-thread tables. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified in QEMU: 4 tids, counter **8000/8000 → OK**, heap **→ OK**, **fds → OK**, and `pstorm 30` still **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.8.90 | **SMP stage 1 — the application processors are alive.** Until now the kernel was single-CPU *in practice*: no test harness ever passed `-smp`, so the AP path shipped in v5.7.x had never actually run past `cli; hlt`. This stage gives each core a real existence — it executes kernel code and services **its own** interrupts — without letting it near the scheduler. **Per-CPU block:** `cpu_info_t` gains `ticks`, and `cpu_self()` resolves the calling core from CPUID(1).EBX[31:24] (an instruction, not a memory access, so it is valid inside an ISR and before any mapping is reachable). **AP bring-up** (`ap_main`): load the BSP's GDT and IDT — both are read-only after boot, so one copy serves every core — then enable the LAPIC and start its **periodic LAPIC timer**, then `for(;;) sti; hlt`. **The TSS is deliberately NOT loaded**: an AP never leaves ring 0 yet, and `ltr` on the shared TSS would #GP anyway since the BSP's `ltr` already set its busy bit. **A dedicated interrupt path, not `irq_common`:** the AP timer gets its own vector (0x40) and stub, because `irq_common` drives the scheduler through the machine-wide globals `saved_rsp`/`next_rsp`/`next_cr3` — an AP entering it would overwrite whatever context switch the BSP was mid-way through. `ap_timer_tick()` therefore does exactly two CPU-local things: bump `cpu_self()->ticks` and EOI its own LAPIC. Nothing else is safe while the kernel still has **no spinlocks** (`preempt_disable()` is just a counter, which means nothing to another core). `enable_smep_smap()` was split so APs call a **silent** `cpu_apply_smep_smap()` — `printf` is shared unlocked state. **Latent bug found and fixed by doing this:** the AP trampoline set only `EFER.LME`, never **`EFER.NXE`**, which `init_paging` enables on the BSP alone. With NXE clear, every PTE carrying `PAGE_NX` is a *reserved-bit* violation — so an AP died the instant it touched its own LAPIC (`map_mmio` maps that page NX). Invisible while APs only halted; instantly fatal once they do anything. **Observability:** `cpus` now prints each core's clock source and tick count. `run.ps1` defaults to `-smp 4`. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified in QEMU with `-smp 4`: 3 APs started / 4 CPUs online, all three AP counters climbing **independently** across two samples (~500 Hz each, against the BSP's 1000 Hz PIT), `threads` still **8000/8000 OK** and `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. **Next:** stage 2 = real spinlocks for the allocators/process table plus per-CPU `user_cr3`/`user_rsp`; stage 3 = per-CPU scheduler, user processes on APs, TLB shootdown IPIs. |
| v5.8.91 | **SMP stage 2 — real spinlocks, and the syscall path's scratch state goes per-CPU.** Stage 1 left the APs alive but fenced off, because everything they might touch was unsynchronised. Two halves fix that. **(1) Locks.** `spinlock.h` adds `spin_lock`/`spin_trylock`/`spin_lock_irqsave` — a plain test-and-set with `pause`, not a ticket lock (8 cores, critical sections of a few dozen instructions; fairness isn't worth the state). The physical page allocator (`alloc_page`/`free_page`/`page_incref`) and `kmalloc`/`kfree` now take one, replacing `preempt_disable()`, which stops a context switch on the LOCAL core and means nothing to another one. Always taken with interrupts off: a spinlock guards against other cores, not against this core re-entering through an interrupt, which would just deadlock. One lock covers slab **and** heap because they are one dependency graph (`slab_new_page` → `alloc_page`), which also makes the ordering trivially deadlock-free — `kmalloc_lock` before `page_lock`, never the reverse. **(2) Per-CPU syscall state.** `user_rsp`/`kernel_rsp`/`user_cr3`/`syscall_frame_ptr` were three `dq 0` globals in isr_stubs.asm — one set for the whole machine, which two cores in a syscall would have overwritten for each other (the v5.8.83 wild-writer class, multiplied). They now live in an **ABI-fixed prefix** of `cpu_info_t` that `syscall_entry` reaches through **GS**, the only register-free anchor the hardware offers; a compile-time offset check in smp.h fails the build if anyone reorders them. **Deliberately NO `swapgs`:** the textbook discipline swaps GS on every ring transition and is wrong here, because `irq_common` enters from a process in ring 0 and can `iretq` into a *different* process in ring 3 — the swaps wouldn't pair, and the next `syscall` would compute gs:0 and write to address 0. Instead GS_BASE simply stays pointed at the per-CPU block in both rings, which is safe only because NyxOS gives userspace no GS base of its own (no TLS, no `arch_prctl`, CR4.FSGSBASE off) and ring 3 still faults on the supervisor page. **If userspace ever gets TLS, this must become a real swapgs.** The 87 C references keep their exact shape via accessor macros in kernel.h. **Honest limit:** per-CPU fixes cross-CPU clobbering, not the save/restore dances around every blocking wait — those slots are still shared between processes time-slicing on one core, and ending that needs them saved into the process at context-switch time. **Bug found during the work:** `cpu_install_gs_base` first wrote MSR `0xC0000100`, which is **FS_BASE**, not GS_BASE (`0xC0000101`) — GS stayed 0, so every user process died on its first syscall while the kernel-only paths (login, compositor, `smpstress`) ran fine. **New `smpstress [secs]`** is what proves the locks: every core runs the same alloc/verify/free loop at once, each checking that the memory it was handed still holds what it wrote. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified with `-smp 4`: **~900k ops across 4 cores, 0 integrity failures**, free pages exactly balanced on a warm run (a cold run is legitimately one page short — a new slab page is never returned), `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**, per-CPU ticks still climbing independently, and **single-CPU boot unaffected**. |
| v5.8.92 | **SMP stage 3a — the APs run scheduled kernel threads, in parallel with the BSP.** Stages 1 and 2 gave the cores life and safe shared state; this one gives them *work*. `ap_timer_stub` stops being a tick counter and becomes a **real context-switching ISR**: it saves the full register set, parks its RSP in the per-CPU `sc_saved_rsp`, calls this core's own `ap_scheduler_tick()`, and resumes whatever that returns in `sc_next_rsp`. The pushed frame matches `init_task_stack()` exactly (error, int_no, 15 GPRs), which is precisely what makes a thread built by the existing `create_process` resumable on an AP. **Mutual exclusion is by PINNING, not by locking the tasks:** `process_t` gains `sched_cpu` (0 = BSP, the memset default, so every existing task is unaffected; N = that AP exclusively). No core will even look at a task that isn't its own, so two cores can never pick the same one and no "running on" flag is needed — the BSP's delicate `irq_scheduler_tick` needed exactly **one** added line (`if (p->sched_cpu != 0) continue;`). A new `sched_lock` guards only the SHAPE of `process_table` (the entries and `process_count`) against a core scanning while another appends or swap-removes; the contents of a `process_t` need no lock because one CPU owns it. **`saved_rsp`/`next_rsp`/`next_cr3` moved into the per-CPU block** and `irq_common` now reaches them through GS — which also *deleted* the old hand-rolled `mov rax, sym / add KERNEL_BASE` dance, since the GS base is already the higher-half alias. That path runs 1000×/second under every existing test, so the change is heavily exercised rather than merely written. Each CPU's scheduler cursor is `sched_cur`, with NULL meaning "the idle context this core booted into", whose stack pointer is parked in `idle_rsp` so there is always somewhere to return when nothing is runnable. No CR3 switching and no ring crossing on APs: these are kernel threads sharing the kernel address space, which is what keeps stage 3a clear of the ring-3 machinery (per-CPU TSS and friends come with 3b). **New `smpthreads [secs]`** wakes one worker per AP. The proof is deliberately un-fakeable: a worker counts into `cpu_self()->work_ops`, i.e. the core that **actually executed it** — if pinning broke and the BSP picked one up, the work would appear in the BSP's row. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified with `-smp 4`: `apworker1/2/3` ran **30.9M / 28.0M / 25.7M** iterations on CPUs 1/2/3 while the **BSP counted 0** (it was sleeping) → `PARALLEL OK`; `smpstress` still ~470k ops / 0 integrity failures / balanced; `threads` **8000/8000 OK**; `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**; and **single-CPU boot unaffected**. **Next (3b):** per-CPU TSS + user processes on the APs + TLB shootdown IPIs. |
| v5.8.93 | **SMP stage 3b — user processes run in ring 3 on the application processors.** The arc closes: cores alive (3a: kernel threads) now take real ring3 -> ring0 -> ring3 transitions of their own. **Per-CPU TSS** was the blocker. A TSS may be busy on only one core — `ltr` sets the busy bit in its descriptor — so cores cannot share one, which is exactly why an AP deliberately skipped `ltr` back in stage 1. The GDT now carries **one 16-byte TSS descriptor per CPU** (`TSS_SEL_FOR(cpu)`, CPU 0 keeping the historical `TSS_SEL`), each core `ltr`s its own, and `tss_set_stack()` writes the CALLING core's RSP0 — which is what makes a ring-3 entry land on the kernel stack of the process *that core* is running. IST stacks are still shared and set on every TSS: two cores double-faulting at the same instant would collide, an accepted limit of an already-fatal path. `ap_timer_stub` gains the CR3 switch it did not need for kernel threads — an AP can now be interrupted on a user CR3, where the kernel's low `-mcmodel=large` code is not mapped at all. **The scheduler side needed no new logic at all:** `ap_scheduler_tick` simply calls **`sched_target()`**, the BSP's existing aiming function, because every slot it writes (`next_rsp`/`next_cr3`/`kernel_rsp` and the TSS RSP0) became per-CPU in stages 2 and 3a. Reusing it rather than writing a second copy is what brings along every ring/CR3 subtlety earlier Heisenbugs taught us. **Placement:** `create_user_process` hands new processes out round-robin when `smp_user_balance` is on — **off by default**, so the default placement stays CPU 0 and enabling SMP costs nothing until asked for. **Thread groups are pinned to one core** (`do_clone` inherits the leader's `sched_cpu`): threads share a PML4, so two on different cores would leave stale TLB entries on an unmap and NyxOS has no shootdown IPI yet. Separate processes own their tables and spread freely. **New `smpuser [n]`** spawns balanced copies of `/spin.elf` and reports where each landed plus what each core is executing. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified with `-smp 4`: `USERSPACE ON APs OK` — processes placed 3/4 on APs, an AP's `sched_cur` observed as the ring-3 `spin` process, and its repeated `[spin] ring-3 process alive` output proving syscalls leaving and re-entering ring 3 **on an AP through its own TSS**; a core seen time-slicing between a kernel thread and a user process; `smpthreads` still `PARALLEL OK`; `smpstress` ~470k ops / 0 integrity failures / balanced; `threads` **8000/8000 OK**; `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**; **single-CPU boot unaffected**. **Remaining gap:** TLB shootdown IPIs — until they exist, thread-group pinning is what keeps the machine honest, and the VFS/terminal are still unlocked against an AP that calls into them. |
| v5.8.94 | **TLB shootdown IPIs — the mechanism, proven.** Changing a page table entry only invalidates the TLB of the core that did it; every other core keeps using a translation the tables no longer describe. `tlb_shootdown(vaddr)` invalidates locally, broadcasts vector **0x41** with the LAPIC's "all excluding self" shorthand, and waits for every online core to acknowledge. **Deadlock was the whole design problem, in two shapes.** (1) Two cores shooting down at once: the sender holds a lock and waits for acks, so if the second core spun on that lock with interrupts *off* it could never service the first's IPI and both would hang. The shootdown lock is therefore taken with plain `spin_lock()` — **deliberately not `spin_lock_irqsave`** — and the ack wait runs with interrupts on, so a core waiting its turn stays interruptible. (2) Shooting down while holding a lock others spin on with interrupts off: **THE RULE is never to call this holding `page_lock` or `kmalloc_lock`**, which callers satisfy because the allocators take and release those internally. The wait is also bounded — a wedged core must not hang the machine. The handler stub switches to the kernel tables before calling C (it can land on a core running ring 3, where the low `-mcmodel=large` code is unmapped) and restores the interrupted CR3 on the way out; it saves **RBX** too, which is callee-saved and clobbered building that address. **The test is built to be able to fail, and the first version correctly refused to claim proof.** `tlbtest` remaps a page and asks CPU 1 what it sees *before* any shootdown: if that read is already fresh, the machine never needed the IPI and the result is reported **INCONCLUSIVE** rather than PASS — which is exactly what happened first time. The reason was real and worth recording: **an AP reloads CR3 on every timer tick (a context switch), and a CR3 reload flushes the TLB**, so a stale entry evaporated within a millisecond. The fix was to have CPU 1 hold the measurement window with **interrupts disabled** (no preemption, no CR3 reload), re-enabling them before the shootdown phase since an IPI cannot reach a core that is not listening. With that window the readings are unambiguous: baseline `0xAAAA…`, after remap with no shootdown **`0xAAAA…` (genuinely stale)**, after the IPI `0xBBBB…` → **SHOOTDOWN PROVEN**. **Scope, stated plainly: this ships the mechanism, not its integration.** `tlb_shootdown` is not yet called from `do_munmap`/`do_mprotect`/the COW remap, and thread groups are still pinned to one core — un-pinning is only safe once every page-table mutation site shoots down, which is the next step. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified with `-smp 4`: `SHOOTDOWN PROVEN`, `USERSPACE ON APs OK`, `PARALLEL OK`, smpstress **521k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm` **330 iterations / 0 faulted**, 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC. One run out of three that included `smpuser` logged a single unexplained `[fault]` on a ring-3 process being SIGKILLed; the baseline suite is clean across runs and it has not reproduced. |
| v5.8.95 | **TLB shootdown wired into unmap and mprotect — and a deadlock shape found while auditing.** v5.8.94 built the mechanism; this makes the kernel actually use it. **The audit changed the plan.** Every candidate call site — `do_munmap`, `do_mprotect`, the COW remap — runs with **interrupts disabled**: syscalls under `SF_MASK` (which clears IF) and the fault handlers behind interrupt gates. That is deadlock shape (1) in disguise, because a sender that cannot take an interrupt cannot acknowledge anyone *else's* shootdown, and two cores unmapping at once would each wait on the other. `tlb_shootdown()` therefore now **enables interrupts for its duration and restores the caller's IF exactly** on the way out; it is never called from an IRQ handler, only from syscall/fault context where being interruptible is already normal. **Which sites got it, and why only those.** Shootdown went into the paths that REMOVE or RESTRICT a mapping — `vm_free_range` (unmap: another core would keep reading a frame already returned to the allocator), `vm_protect_range` (mprotect only ever narrows what a core has cached) and `vm_unmap`. The demand-fault paths that make a not-present page present keep their plain local `invlpg`: a remote core holding the old not-present state simply faults and re-walks, so a broadcast would buy nothing. **Deliberately NOT done, and this is the honest part: thread groups stay pinned to one core.** Un-pinning needs the COW remap covered too, and that one lives in the `#PF` handler — where enabling interrupts is exactly the kind of change that produces hangs which do not reproduce headless. Shipping the pin removal without verifying that would be selling safety that was never tested. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified with `-smp 4` with the shootdown live: `pstorm` exercises `vm_free_range` on every process teardown, so the new path runs constantly — smpstress **533k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. **Next:** cover the COW remap safely, then un-pin thread groups. |
| v5.8.96 | **COW remap shoots down too — and two things that did NOT work, reverted and documented.** The kept change is small and verified: the copy-on-write path in `vm_handle_fault` REPLACES an existing translation (a shared read-only frame becomes a private writable one), so a sibling thread on another core holding the old entry would keep reading the stale frame. It now calls `tlb_shootdown(cr2)` instead of a local `invlpg`, reached with interrupts off, which `tlb_shootdown` handles by enabling them for its duration and restoring the caller's IF. **What failed #1 — un-pinning thread groups.** This was the increment's headline and it does not work: with threads spread across cores, `threads` starts all four tids and the machine **wedges** — no counter, no output, no fault, no panic. Reverted to pinning. The diagnosis worth keeping: TLB shootdown covers the page tables, but what it does not cover is that the blocking primitives a thread group leans on — futex parking, and the per-CPU `user_cr3`/`user_rsp` save/restore around every wait — were written for one scheduler on one core. That, not stale TLBs, is the real prerequisite. **What failed #2 — a correction to v5.8.93.** Testing `pstorm` with `smp_user_balance` ON (which no earlier test had ever done — `smpuser` cleared the flag before pstorm ran) shows it **wedges too**. So the balancing shipped in v5.8.93 is safe for long-lived processes and NOT for fork/exec churn; `smpbalance` now prints that warning rather than implying otherwise. The cause is again not the page tables. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified in the DEFAULT configuration with the COW shootdown live: smpstress **465k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**; and with balancing on, `threads` still 8000/8000 with heap and fds OK. **Next, and it is now clear what it is:** make the blocking primitives (futex, waitpid, the per-CPU syscall-slot save/restore) correct across cores. Un-pinning threads and balancing churn both depend on that, not on more shootdown work. |
| v5.8.97 | **`get_current_process()` was BSP-only — the root cause of both v5.8.96 hangs, found and fixed.** The question every syscall asks first is "which process is running right now?", and the answer came from `current_idx`, which is written **solely** by `irq_scheduler_tick` — the BSP's scheduler. `ap_scheduler_tick` (v5.8.92) tracks its own task in `cpu_info[].sched_cur` and never touches `current_idx`. So any task executing on an application processor resolved to **whatever the BSP happened to be running**. A thread calling `futex_wait` on an AP therefore blocked the BSP's task instead of its own, and the machine wedged with no fault and no panic — exactly the symptom that cost two increments. It stayed hidden because work that never asks who it is (`spin.elf` writing to serial, which is what v5.8.93 verified with) looked perfectly healthy. The fix is five lines: on an AP, return that core's `sched_cur` (NULL there means the idle context, i.e. genuinely no current process); on the BSP, the old path. **This was found by reasoning about the code, not by debugging** — the two hangs shared "quiet, no fault, only under cross-core scheduling", which points at identity, not memory. **Result: the wedge is gone.** With threads spread across cores `threads` now completes. **It is still not enough to un-pin, and the pin stays:** the next failure is immediate and much more informative — `pstorm` panics with **#GP (err 0x8) at RIP 0x10049f**, inside the interrupt-stub / ring-transition path, after two iterations. That is the next thing to chase, and unlike a silent wedge it comes with an address. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified in the default configuration: 4-CPU smpstress **435k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**; single-CPU **PASS** as well. |
| v5.8.98 | **The #GP pinned down to one instruction — but not solved, and said so.** `objdump` puts the panic RIP `0x10049f` exactly on the **`iretq` that closes `irq_common`**, the BSP's interrupt return. Error code `0x8` says the frame it pops names selector `0x08`, i.e. a bad CS/SS. The hypothesis chased: `irq_scheduler_tick` does `cur->stack = saved_rsp` on `process_table[current_idx]` **without checking affinity**, and `current_idx` really can come to point at an AP-pinned task without this scheduler ever choosing one — the reap paths swap-remove and then patch `current_idx` to the moved slot, which can hold anything. `pstorm` forks and reaps constantly, which fits the panic arriving two iterations in. **The guard is kept** (`if (cur->sched_cpu == 0)`), and it is a **no-op in the default configuration** where every task has `sched_cpu == 0` — so it costs nothing and is a genuine safety net once balancing is on. **But it did not fix it.** With threads spread, the same `iretq` still faults; the error code moved from `0x8` to **`0x0`**, which is not selector-related at all (a non-canonical RIP or bad RFLAGS in the frame). So the frame reaching that instruction is wrong in more than one way and the cause is upstream of both fixes so far. Thread groups stay pinned; reverted for the third time. **What the next session gets that this one did not have:** the exact instruction, both error codes and what each rules in or out, and a reliable reproducer (`smpbalance on`, then `threads`, then `pstorm`). This is now a job for `-icount` + a gdb watchpoint on the outgoing task's `stack` field — the v5.8.83 methodology — not for more reasoning from the source. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified in the default configuration: smpstress **480k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.8.99 | **`preempt_count` is per-CPU (release blocker P0.2) — and a correction to my own priority call.** It was one `static volatile int` shared by every core, which was wrong in both directions once a second core scheduled: the BSP's `preempt_disable()` meant nothing to an AP, and an AP raising it would have stopped the BSP's scheduler outright (`irq_scheduler_tick` returns early while it is non-zero). It now lives in `cpu_info_t`, because "don't preempt me" is inherently a statement about **one** core. `ap_scheduler_tick` honours its own count too — expressed as "keep running the current task" rather than an early return, because `sc_next_rsp` **must** be written before control goes back to the stub or the core resumes the previous tick's stale pointer. **The correction:** I ranked this P0 ("reachable in the default configuration") and an audit says that was too strong. The only real `preempt_disable` callers are `net.c`, `pipe.c` and the reap paths, all of which run on the BSP today; **no AP-reachable path calls it** (memory.c's uses became spinlocks in v5.8.91). So the harmful direction was **latent, not active** — it would have become real the moment any AP work called it, which the P1 locking sweep would have triggered. Worth shipping as a footgun removed, not as a live bug fixed. **What this deliberately does NOT fix, and the comment in process.c says so:** call sites using `preempt_disable` as a cross-core *lock* (net.c, pipe.c, reap_zombies) are still only safe while one core touches that state — true by default, false once user processes run on APs. Those need real spinlocks and remain P1. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **489k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**; single-CPU **PASS**. |
| v5.9.0-rc1 | **Release blocker P0.3 closed: the last local-only invalidations on paths that REMOVE or REPLACE a mapping.** Two sites, deliberately treated differently. **`unmap_page()`** (paging.c) removes an entry from the KERNEL tables, which every core shares — the case v5.8.95 missed while wiring up `vm_free_range`/`vm_protect_range`/`vm_unmap`. It now shoots down; its single caller (`vbe.c`, at mode-set) runs on the BSP holding no allocator lock, so the deadlock rule is satisfied. **`map_pml4()`** is the subtle one, because it serves two situations that must not be conflated: REPLACING a live translation (other cores hold the old one — broadcast required; this is exactly what `tlbtest`'s remap needed) versus creating one where there was none (a remote core holding the old not-present state simply faults and re-walks — a broadcast buys nothing). Since the second case **is the demand-fault path**, shooting down unconditionally would have put an IPI on every page fault in the system. So it now branches on whether the old PTE was present. **A consequence found while testing, and fixed:** with `map_pml4` shooting down, `tlbtest` triggers one while CPU 1 deliberately sits with interrupts disabled — a core that cannot answer. The ack wait's bound was 100,000,000 spins, which meant the sender burned seconds waiting for an answer that would never come. Cut to 2,000,000, and the timeout now **prints once** instead of degrading in silence (release blocker P3.2). **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified at 4 CPUs: smpstress **492k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**, and `tlbtest` still reports **SHOOTDOWN PROVEN** with a genuinely stale read in the middle. **Not verified:** that the new timeout warning actually fires — the test harness filters serial output and would not have shown it. |
| v5.9.0-rc2 | **Ring-3 → ring-0 arbitrary write closed at its root (3 CRITICAL findings, one fix).** A full-codebase audit (12 subsystems, adversarially verified) found three separate privilege escalations that all reduced to ONE primitive. `alloc_page_directory()` mirrors `kernel_pml4[511]` — the identity map of **all** physical RAM — into every user PML4, and `KERNEL_BASE` is exactly `-2^39`, so `(KERNEL_BASE + X) >> 39 & 0x1FF` is 511 and the walk in `user_v2p()` resolved `KERNEL_BASE+X` straight to physical `X`. `user_v2p` had **no address bound and no PAGE_USER check**, so every `copy_to_user` whose destination was not separately validated was an arbitrary write into kernel memory from an ordinary unprivileged program. Reachable through at least: `signal_dispatch`'s trampoline write to the raw user RSP (with an attacker-chosen value, via `signal()`'s third argument → ring-0 code execution), `signal_deliver_fault`'s identical write, and `do_sigprocmask`'s unvalidated `oldset` pointer (a single call with the default empty mask zeroes the adjacent `uid`/`euid` in `process_t`). **Fixed at the choke point, not per call site** — patching each caller would have left the primitive intact for the next unguarded copy. `user_v2p()` now enforces two independent guards: the address must be in the canonical lower half, AND every level of the walk must carry `PAGE_USER`, so a mapping the hardware would refuse to ring 3 is refused here too. The second guard is what keeps this correct if a kernel-only mapping ever appears in the lower half. **New `user/vmtest.c` proves it in both directions**, which is the only way a security test means anything: a legitimate pointer must still be ACCEPTED (a kernel that rejected everything would pass a one-sided test) and four hostile ones — higher-half kernel alias, physical-zero alias, first non-canonical address, null page — must be REFUSED. All five correct: **USER/KERNEL BOUNDARY HOLDS**. Also fixed: `vmtest` surfaced that the userspace `printf` ignores width specifiers (`%-46s` prints literally). **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **458k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. **Still open from the same audit:** 4 HIGH (socket fd confused with a VFS handle in `dup2`/`mmap`; `mprotect` can make the shared libc writable; `vm_protect_range` clears `PTE_COW` and breaks fork isolation) and 12 MEDIUM. |
| v5.9.0-rc3 | **Two more CRITICALs closed, from the completion audit of the six areas whose verification had died on a quota limit.** **(1) `ext2_readdir` stack smash (ext2.c:597).** ext2 allows names up to 255 bytes; `dirent_t.name` is `MAX_FILENAME` (128). The copy was unclamped, so a long name wrote up to 128 bytes past the field and, for the last slot, past the caller's whole array — a kernel stack smash with attacker-chosen bytes. The verifier found this **understated**: no hostile disk image is needed, because `ext2_create` does not bound the name either, so an unprivileged process can plant a 200-character name itself and then trigger the smash through `getdents`. Fixed with a clamp, plus two adjacent defects the verifier flagged in the same loop: a `rec_len < 8` floor (a zero `rec_len` made the scan spin forever) and a check that the name actually lies inside the block that was read. **(2) `elf_load_image` had no user/kernel bound on `p_vaddr` (elf.c:84).** `alloc_page_directory` copies `kernel_pml4[511]` **by value** into every process PML4, so a PT_LOAD with a higher-half `p_vaddr` walked into the page tables the kernel itself runs on — either grafting a ring-3-accessible page at a fixed kernel VA in every address space (never reclaimed, since `free_page_directory` stops below PML4[511]) or writing a PTE into identity-mapped kernel RAM. A user program only had to write a file and `execve` it. Also fixed alongside it: `offset + filesz > size` **overflowed**, so a crafted `p_offset` near 2^64 passed the extent check and copied from outside the image buffer. **`USER_SPACE_MIN`/`USER_SPACE_END` moved from syscall.c to kernel.h** — the user/kernel boundary is not a syscall concept, and the ELF loader needing the same bound is exactly how it drifts. One definition now. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **595k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC** — and pstorm execve's constantly, which is what proves the new ELF bound does not reject legitimate binaries. **Audit status:** 18 areas covered across two passes, 64 confirmed findings total. 22 findings remain UNVERIFIED (the quota limit hit again) — notably several claimed-critical ones in the compositor, editor_win and switch.asm that must be checked before v5.9.0 ships. |
| v5.9.0-rc4 | **The missing `cld` — every interrupt entry path was running with whatever RFLAGS.DF ring 3 left set.** Two findings the completion audit could only claim (its verifiers died on the quota) and which I checked by reading the code; both were real. **(1) No `cld` anywhere except boot.asm.** DF is part of ring 3's state and survives into a handler, so a user program can execute `std` and simply wait for a timer interrupt. Every rep-string in the kernel then runs BACKWARDS — and `memcpy_asm`/`memset_asm` in stubs.asm are literally `rep movsb` / `rep stosb`, so effectively every copy and clear in the kernel would walk down through memory instead of up. The SysV ABI also requires DF clear on entry to any C function, so compiler-emitted string ops assume it too. The `syscall` path was already covered (IA32_FMASK clears IF and DF); interrupts and exceptions were covered by nothing. Fixed by putting `cld` in the **SAVE_REGS macro**, which covers irq_common, isr_common and ap_timer_stub at once so a future stub cannot forget it, plus tlb_ipi_stub which does not use the macro. **(2) `switch_context` (switch.asm) was thoroughly broken.** The audit spotted that it pushed 14 registers and popped 15, so resuming a context it had itself saved consumed the return address as if it were RAX and `ret` jumped to whatever sat above. Fixing it exposed a second, unreported bug: with 14 pushes the saved RDI/RSI sit at +72/+80, but the code read **+96/+104** — i.e. it took `rcx` and `rbx` as its two arguments. Both fixed and the frame documented. It is **dead code** (the preemptive scheduler switches through irq_common's saved_rsp/next_rsp; nothing calls it), which is why neither bug had ever been observed — but a broken context switcher sitting in a kernel is a trap for whoever wires it up next. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **607k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.9.0-rc5 | **The mprotect family: fork isolation restored, and the address range bounded (3 HIGH + 1 MEDIUM).** **(1) `vm_protect_range` destroyed copy-on-write.** It rebuilt each PTE from the frame address alone, which dropped `PTE_COW` and, for `PROT_WRITE`, granted `PAGE_WRITABLE` outright. So after `fork()`, an `mprotect(PROT_WRITE)` on shared memory let one process write the frame the other was still reading — no fault, no copy, **silent cross-process corruption**, which is precisely the isolation `fork()` exists to provide. Now a COW page keeps its marker and is denied the write bit, so the first write still faults into the COW handler, privatises the page, and only then becomes writable. `PTE_DEMAND` is preserved for the same reason. **(2) `do_mprotect` and `do_munmap` accepted ANY address and ANY length.** Aimed at the shared-libc mapping, mprotect made those pages writable for one process — and since that is a single physical copy mapped read-only into every process, it is a **shared-library patch visible system-wide**. Aimed at a higher-half address, both walked the kernel's own page tables. With a huge length, the per-4 KB loop ran for billions of iterations with interrupts masked — an unprivileged hard hang. All three fall to one bound, applied to both functions, using the same `USER_SPACE_MIN`/`USER_SPACE_END` that `user_v2p` and the ELF loader now share from kernel.h. Wrap-around (`end <= addr`) is rejected too. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **609k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC** — pstorm forks and COWs continuously, which is what shows the COW-preservation change does not break legitimate sharing. |
| v5.9.0-rc6 | **Three of the unverified findings checked by hand — all three real, all three fixed.** **(1) `tss_entry_t` had twelve reserved bytes where Intel Vol 3 Fig 7-11 specifies eight** (a leftover of the 32-bit SS0/SS1/SS2 fields). Every IST slot therefore sat four bytes too high: `tss_set_ist()` wrote IST1 at offset 40 while the CPU reads it from 36, so the double-fault handler's stack pointer was always garbage and a double fault triple-faulted into a reboot instead of reaching the panic screen. **The IST mechanism had never worked.** Struct corrected to the spec layout (104 bytes, iomap_base at 102). Note RSP0 is at offset 4 and was never affected, which is why ring transitions always worked and this stayed invisible. **(2) `window_create` never zeroed the window_t it kmalloc'd.** Every field the function does not explicitly assign was heap garbage — including the `on_pressed`/`on_key` callback pointers, which the compositor guards with `if (win->on_pressed)` before calling. A non-NULL garbage value passes that guard and is then **called**, so opening a window could jump to an arbitrary address. Now zeroed before the field assignments. **(3) `editor_save()` put a 128 KB buffer on a 4 KB kernel stack.** `char buf[EDITOR_MAX_LINES * EDITOR_LINE_LEN]` is 512 × 256 = 131072 bytes; kernel task stacks are `kmalloc(4096)`. Every single Save in the Text Editor overflowed the kernel stack by a factor of thirty-two. Moved to the heap, with the two `sizeof(buf)` bounds rewritten (they would have become `sizeof(char*)` = 8) and a matching `kfree`. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **603k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. **Caveat on (1):** the suite exercises RSP0 on every syscall, so the ring-transition half of the TSS is well covered, but the IST path itself is only reachable from an actual double fault and is therefore NOT directly tested — the fix rests on the layout matching the manual. |
| v5.9.0-rc7 | **Three more HIGHs: framebuffer clipping and the dynamic linker.** **(1) `fb_blit()` had no bounds check of any kind.** It wrote `h` rows of `w` pixels at `(dx, dy)` without ever comparing against `fb_width`/`fb_height`, so any caller blitting near the right or bottom edge — a window dragged there, Paint's 512x384 canvas — wrote straight past the end of the framebuffer into whatever the allocator had placed after it. Now clipped, with the subtlety that matters: the copy is narrowed but the SOURCE STRIDE is not, since source rows remain the original width apart. Off-screen negative coordinates arrive as huge unsigned values and are caught by the same test. **(2) `do_dlopen` kept a raw pointer into the VFS node's buffer** (`dynlibs[slot].elf = data`, justified by a comment reading "ramdisk data persists"). That holds for initramfs files and for nothing else: `dlopen("/tmp/x.so")` followed by `unlink()`, a `write()`, or an `open(O_TRUNC)` frees or reallocs that buffer, and `do_dlsym` then walks freed memory on behalf of whoever calls it. The registry outlives the file, so it now takes its own kmalloc'd copy. **(3) `do_dlsym` trusted almost every field of a user-writable file.** Only `e_shoff`/`e_shnum` and `sh_link` were validated; `sh_offset`, `sh_size` and `st_name` were taken on trust, so a crafted `.so` made the symbol loop read wherever it liked and `strcmp` walk off the end of the image. All three are now bounded against `elf_size`, and the string table is required to be NUL-terminated at its end — which is precisely what makes an in-range `st_name` safe to hand to `strcmp`. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: `dltest` resolves all three symbols through the hardened path (`dlopen -> handle 1`, `demo_name`, `demo_gcd(48,36)=12`, `demo_fib(10)=55`, **OK**); 4-CPU smpstress **607k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.9.0-rc8 | **`dup()` of a mount-backed file was a double free.** A VFS fd IS the `vfs_node_t*`, and `dup` aliases it — which `SYS_DUP`'s own comment justified with "vfs_close is a no-op for the ramdisk nodes ring 3 opens, so closing either fd later is safe". That is true for ramdisk nodes and **false for a mount-backed mirror**, which `vfs_close` frees: `->data` was `kfree`d twice and the node returned to the pool twice, after which the pool hands the same node to two unrelated opens. Note `dup2` was already correct — it MOVES a VFS handle precisely to avoid this, and the comment there says so; only `dup`, which must leave both fds open, was exposed. Fixed with an alias count on the mirror (`dup_refs`): `vfs_dup()` on the aliasing path, and `vfs_close` releases only when the last holder closes. Sockets and pipes are untouched — pipes were already refcounted. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **623k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK** with heap and shared fds intact, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. **Caveat:** the regression suite runs diskless, so it exercises the ramdisk path rather than a real `/mnt` mirror — this fix is verified as non-breaking, not as exercised. A `-hda` run doing `dup` on a `/mnt` file would close that gap. |
| v5.9.0-rc10 | **`libseg_load` — the last confirmed CRITICAL — validated nothing at all.** It is the FIRST code to touch a shared-object image, running before both `do_dlopen` and `do_dlsym`, which the earlier passes hardened; `dlopen()` will happily be aimed at a `.so` the calling process wrote itself. The damage was not subtle: the segment copy reads from `data + offset` with **`p_offset` never compared against `size`**, so a crafted program header copies arbitrary kernel memory into a frame that `libseg_map` then maps INTO THE PROCESS — an arbitrary kernel-memory disclosure readable straight from ring 3. Nothing else was checked either: the program-header table could sit outside the image, `e_phentsize` was unvalidated, `p_filesz` could exceed `p_memsz`, and `p_vaddr` was unbounded — and since `libseg_map` installs these frames into the process PML4 whose higher half mirrors the kernel's own tables by value, a higher-half `p_vaddr` writes into kernel page tables, the same class already fixed in `elf_load_image`. Now bounded on all five axes, with every comparison written subtraction-first so the arithmetic cannot wrap. **This completes the audit's critical set: all 6 confirmed criticals across both passes are closed.** **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified — and the verification matters here because `libseg_load` maps the SHARED LIBC at boot, so bounds that were too strict would stop the machine dead rather than fail quietly: `[LIBC] shared libc mapped at 0x30000000` still succeeds, `dltest` resolves all three symbols through the hardened path (**OK**), 4-CPU smpstress **472k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.9.0-rc11 | **Ring-3 flags that survived into the kernel, and the rest of the ELF header validation (1 HIGH + 3 more).** **`IA32_FMASK` cleared only IF and DF.** Everything else in RFLAGS is ring-3 state that rode straight into kernel code. Now also cleared: **TF** — a syscall made with single-step set raised a #DB on the very first kernel instruction, a ring-0 debug exception from an unprivileged program at a moment of its choosing; **NT**, which corrupts IRET behaviour; and **AC**, which matters most because AC is the flag that **suspends SMAP** — the kernel enables SMAP (v5.8.86) precisely so a stray ring-0 access to a user page traps, and letting ring 3 hand us AC=1 switched that protection off for the whole syscall. This is the same family as the `cld` fix one level up: the syscall path was covered for DF and nothing else. **ELF header validation, three more holes.** (1) `e_phentsize` was never checked, while the bound test computes from it and the `phdr[i]` indexing strides by `sizeof(elf64_phdr_t)` — `e_phentsize=1` with `e_phnum=1000` passes a 1000-byte check and then reads 56000. Now required to equal the struct size exactly. (2) The bound's multiply had already been widened to 64-bit, but the **addition still wrapped**, so a huge `e_phoff` sailed past; rewritten subtraction-first. (3) `e_entry` was never validated — and it ends up in an iretq frame, so a non-canonical or higher-half entry point does not kill the process, it faults **the return to user in ring 0**: a kernel panic triggered by running a crafted file. (4) `p_memsz` had no practical ceiling; the user-space bound still permits terabytes, and the loop allocates a frame per page with no way out, so one crafted PT_LOAD walked off with every free page in the system. Capped at `ELF_MAX_SEGMENT` (256 MB). **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified — and these changes fail loudly rather than quietly, since a wrong FMASK bit or a too-tight entry bound stops every binary from running: `[LIBC] shared libc mapped`, `dltest` **OK**, 4-CPU smpstress **466k ops / 0 integrity failures** / balanced, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**, single-CPU **PASS**. |
| v5.9.0-rc12 | **The 22-pixel GUI family: one missing term, six places.** A window's (x, y) is the top-left of its TITLE BAR and its content starts `TITLE_H` = 22 px below. The compositor already had `win_client_y()` for exactly this — but `static inline` inside compositor.c, so no window module could use it. Every hit-test handler re-derived the origin from `win->y` and forgot the title bar, which is why clicks in Paint, the Text Editor and Sound Test all landed exactly 22 px above what was drawn. Fixed at the root: `WIN_CLIENT_X`/`WIN_CLIENT_Y` are now exported from compositor.h, so the draw path and the hit tests share ONE definition of where content begins instead of each module being right or wrong on its own. **The audit reported three sites; the sweep found six.** Paint had two (click AND mousemove, so strokes landed offset as well), and two more were never reported at all: `editor_win.c:276` — the editor's TEXT AREA, not just its toolbar, so clicking to place the cursor was off by the same 22 px — and `calc_win.c:165`, the calculator's entire button grid. **Two modules were already correct** (`fileman_win`, `minesweeper_win` spelled out `win->y + TITLE_H` by hand); they were converted to the macro so the invariant is stated once rather than re-derived. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **629k ops / 0 integrity failures**, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC** — the compositor boots and the desktop renders. **Honest limit:** the regression suite is headless and does not click, so this is verified as non-breaking and correct BY CONSTRUCTION (draw and hit-test now expand from the same macro), not by observing a corrected click. `compositor.c:264`, the start-menu hit test, is a DIFFERENT bug (20 px, plus an item-count off-by-one) and is deliberately left for its own fix. |
| v5.9.0-rc13+rc14 | **The editor no longer eats your file, and the file manager stops indexing entries[-1].** **`edit` (user/edit.c) truncated silently in THREE independent places** — an 8192-byte load buffer, `MAXLINES` 200, `MAXCOL` 200 — and then `save()` opened the original with `O_CREAT | O_TRUNC` and wrote back only what had survived. Opening a 15 KB file and pressing ^O destroyed it. The chosen fix is to **refuse the save**, not to raise the limits: the editor cannot know which part of the file mattered, so refusing is the only honest behaviour. Three details that decide whether the fix actually works: the check runs **before** the `open`, because `O_TRUNC` empties the file the instant it succeeds and a later check would already have destroyed what it protects; the warning is a **persistent** `[READ-ONLY]` marker in the status bar rather than a transient message, since the main loop clears `msg` on any keystroke and the user would otherwise only find out when saving; and the two new strings were **cut to fit `msg[48]`** — at 53 and 60 characters they would have been a `strcpy` overflow, which would have been an ironic way to fix a data-loss bug. **Proven end to end:** `cp /libc.so /big.txt` (15024 bytes), open in `edit`, press ^O — the status bar shows `[READ-ONLY]` and `REFUSED: truncated on load, would lose data`, and the file is still **15024 bytes** afterwards. **`fileman_win.c` committed a rename through `entries[fm->sel_index]` with no bounds check.** The selection is validated when the rename STARTS, but it can be cleared while the user is still typing the new name — `fileman_refresh()` drops it when a search filter stops matching, and deleting resets it to -1. Committing then read 64 bytes from before the array and handed them to `vfs_rename` as a path. Every other consumer of `sel_index` already guards it (lines 134, 160, 169); the commit path was the one that did not, and now does, cancelling cleanly instead. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Verified: 4-CPU smpstress **520k ops / 0 integrity failures**, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.9.0-rc15 | **The ext2 write path, verified by `e2fsck` instead of by itself.** Nine defects in the one subsystem whose damage outlives a reboot, all the same shape — *a field written without asking how much room it had*. **The allocator handed out the wrong block.** ext2 numbers the block bitmap from `first_data_block`, not from zero; on the 1024-byte-block image NyxOS ships that is 1, and `ext2_alloc_block` used the bit index AS the block number. So it returned block K while marking bit K, which owns block K+1: the block it handed out was one the bitmap still called used (the last inode-table block on a fresh image), and the block it marked used was one nobody held. It *looked* fine because the collision lands on the tail of the inode table, which an almost-empty disk never reaches — a silent overwrite of live metadata waiting for the disk to fill up. `bit_to_block`/`block_to_bit` now convert, and alloc and free always move together. **The 13th block of every large file was zeros.** `ext2_write_file` zeroed the caller's staged data to initialise a fresh indirect block, then wrote those zeros out as the file's data. Fixed by *deleting* the memset — `ext2_alloc_block` already zeroes what it returns. **`de->name` is at +8, not +4**, so `memset(de->name, 0, rec_len - 4)` cleared four bytes past the entry — off the end of the block buffer whenever the entry was the last in its block, which is the common case, because the last entry's `rec_len` runs to the end of the block. **`name_len` is a uint8** fed an unbounded `strlen`, so a 300-byte name recorded itself as 44 while the `memcpy` copied 300. **`ext2_create_file` carried a verbatim copy of `add_dirent_to_parent`**, so every bound bug existed twice and had to be found twice — plus a `links_count++` on the parent for a plain file, which is not how ext2 counts links; it now calls the one implementation. **`free_inode_blocks` read the level-2 indirect block into the buffer its level-1 walk was still iterating** — precisely the aliasing this file's five-buffer split exists to prevent, at the one line the split never reached. **A 4096-byte block buffer lived on the kernel stack**, sized by a guess rather than by `block_size`, in a function that also descends into the ATA layer. **`resolve_path` copied path components into a 256-byte stack array with no ceiling** — the most-travelled function in the driver, reachable from any process that can name a file. **rmdir called a directory empty if no entry had a name longer than two characters**, so a real file called `ab` did not count and the directory was deleted with a live child inside. Mount now validates the geometry it reads off the disk (`log_block_size`, `inodes_per_group`, `inode_size`, the `*_per_group` bit counts against one bitmap block) — each of those fed a divide or a buffer size, so a corrupt image was a `#DE` or an overflow rather than a mount failure. Also closed the consistency gaps `e2fsck` reported: `i_blocks` now charges the inode for its indirect block, deleted inodes get a real `dtime` (from a new RTC-backed `ext2_now()`, so files stop being dated 1970 too), and the superblock free counters and the BGD directory count are actually written to disk — `ext2_sync_superblock()` existed but had **no callers**, so the on-disk totals drifted further from the truth with every write. **Verified against the format, not against NyxOS.** A fresh `mkfs.ext2 -b 1024` image, a workload run inside QEMU, then `e2fsck -fn` and `debugfs` from the host: **e2fsck exit code 0, all five passes clean, zero `Fix?` prompts**, and a 23 KB file copied through the indirect-block path comes back **byte-for-byte identical**. That last check is the point — the `first_data_block` bug both wrote and read back consistently, so no test run from inside the OS could ever have seen it. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Regression: 4-CPU smpstress **535k ops / 0 integrity failures**, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**. |
| v5.9.0-rc16 | **A VFS fd IS a node pointer — so the node pool must know who is holding one.** `vfs_open` returns `(int)(uintptr_t)node`; there is no descriptor table. That single design fact is what made three separate reports one bug: the pool had no idea whether anyone still held a node, so it recycled and wiped them freely. **`vfs_unlink` never returned the node to the pool.** It `memset`'d the struct in place and dropped the pointer — no `free_node`. Every single delete permanently burned one of the 256 slots (root alone holds ~58) until file creation simply stopped working, with no error that pointed anywhere near the cause. It also wiped the struct with no regard for open fds, and an fd here points straight at it. **`proc_sync` handed live `/proc` nodes back to the pool.** It runs from `vfs_open`/`vfs_isdir`, so `cat /proc/<pid>/status` on a process that exits mid-read had its node freed and reissued by the very next open — the reader then read, and could write through, whatever file got the slot. **`free_node` had no double-free guard**, so any path reaching it twice put one node in the free list twice and the next two allocations handed it to two different files. Fixed as one mechanism rather than three patches: an `open_refs` count on every node kind (1 after open, +1 per dup, −1 per close), an `orphaned` flag so a node unlinked while open leaves the tree immediately but is freed by its last close (the unlink-while-open semantics callers already assumed), and an `on_free_list` flag in `free_node`. The old `dup_refs` — which counted only mount mirrors, leaving a dup'd `/proc` handle untracked — is subsumed by `open_refs`. `vfs_unlink` also now **refuses a non-empty directory** instead of dropping the whole subtree on the floor: the directory node went away and every node beneath it stayed allocated but unreachable, which was both a leak and silent data loss. **Measured, not argued.** `vfsfill.elf` fills a directory until the VFS refuses, then unlinks everything it made — its own comment claims this "returns the inode pool so the test can be re-run", an intention the code never honoured. Running it three times is the whole proof. Old code: **128, then 12, then 0** — the pool was dead after two passes and the system could not create another file until reboot. Fixed: **128, 128, 128**, with `/sh.elf` still stating correctly each time. The control run was done by temporarily restoring the old `vfs_unlink` body in place, so the two numbers come from the same harness and the same build path. **No new syscalls** (still 52). Regression: 4-CPU smpstress **614k ops / 0 integrity failures**, `threads` **8000/8000 OK**, `pstorm 30` **0 faulted / 0 MAPCORRUPT / 0 PANIC**, and the rc15 ext2 check still reports **e2fsck exit 0** with the 23 KB file byte-identical. |
| v5.9.0-rc17 | **The three GUI highs — and a fourth found only by actually clicking.** **`gui.c:103` was a divide by zero in ring 0.** The paint loop interpolated between the previous and current pointer positions with `(x1 - x0) * i / steps`, and `steps` is 0 whenever the pointer has not moved since the last sample — i.e. any time the user holds the button down without dragging, which is the most ordinary thing to do with a paint tool. The loop still ran once, so `0 * 0 / 0` raised `#DE` in the kernel. At zero steps the endpoints are the same point, so there is nothing to interpolate; it plots and moves on. **`compositor.c:264` disagreed with the drawing code on all three of its numbers.** Entries start `START_ITEM_Y` = 28 px below the menu top, under the green header, and the hit test subtracted 8 — twenty pixels of skew, so the lower two thirds of every entry selected the one BELOW it and clicking "Terminal" opened Settings. The menu has thirteen entries and the test rejected `>= 12`, so Minesweeper could not be launched from the menu at all (the `case 12:` action existed and was reachable only from a desktop icon). And because C integer division truncates toward ZERO, a click on the header computed index 0 rather than something negative, the `< 0` guard never fired, and clicking the title bar launched File Manager. Fixed the way the TITLE_H family was: `START_HDR_H`/`START_ITEM_Y`/`START_ITEM_H`/`START_ITEM_N` are now read by both the drawing loop and the hit test, instead of each deriving the layout and being right or wrong on its own. **`fb.c` was worse than reported.** `fb_enable_backbuffer()` returned early when a buffer already existed — without repointing `fb_addr` at it. After a logout that pointer is the HARDWARE framebuffer, because `fb_use_lfb_direct()` sent the login screen there, so the *second* desktop of a session drew every element straight onto the visible screen (the flicker double buffering exists to prevent) while `fb_present` kept blitting the back buffer — still holding the previous user's frozen desktop — over it every frame. It now repoints and clears the buffer, so nothing from a session that logged out can survive into the next, and `fb_present` refuses to publish unless drawing is actually going to the back buffer. **The fourth: menu transitions were level-triggered.** The whole left-button block runs under `if (btns & 1)`, which re-runs every compositor iteration while the button is held — right for dragging, wrong for menus. Pressing "Menu" toggled it open on iteration 1; on iteration 2 the button was still down and the pointer was still on the taskbar at y=750, outside the menu rect, so the click-outside branch closed it again. The Start menu opened and shut inside a single press and whether anything survived came down to how long the click lasted. Opening, closing and selecting are all edge events; only the drag paths are level events. **Verified by driving the real PS/2 mouse** through the QEMU monitor and reading frames back as PNGs — the first suite in this project that clicks rather than types. It confirmed the drawn menu geometry matches the new constants exactly (13 entries at `360 + 28i`, Minesweeper last at y≈708) and that a press now opens the menu deterministically where the old build was parity-dependent. **Then verified end to end, by clicking.** The scripted harness could not deliver a button press — QEMU's HMP `mouse_button` does not itself emit a PS/2 packet — so the checks were driven on the real machine instead, with a hybrid that covers each tool's gap: the QEMU monitor positions the guest cursor exactly, and a genuine host press supplies the button. (`left_click` is unusable here: it moves the host pointer first, and under grab that relative motion drags the guest cursor off target. Press and release with no move in between works.) Results, each read off a screenshot: clicking the visual centre of **"Terminal"** (index 3) opened a **Terminal** — the old 20 px skew would have opened Settings; clicking **"Minesweeper"** (index 12), with no Minesweeper window on screen beforehand, **opened one** — the old `>= 12` guard rejected that index outright; clicking the **green header** opened nothing and left the menu up, with **Calculator** (index 11) clicked immediately afterwards as a positive control proving the input path was live at that moment, so "nothing happened" means ignored and not undelivered.
